Stanley Meeker Cazneaux
Stanley Meeker Cazneaux. 127th Grand Master of Masons in California.
was born on August 2. 1933, at San Rafael, California. He was educated in
the public schools of Marin County. and attended the College of Marin at
Kentfield before entering military service during the Korean War.
He served in the United States Navy from 1953 to 1957. including service
on the Admiral's Staff at Pearl Harbor. Honolulu. Hawaii. In 1955 he married
the former Gloria Parcell. They have three children: A daughter. Cheryl
Emerson; and sons David and Richard. along with three grandsons. Matthew and Patrick Emerson. and Ryan Cazneaux. He resides in Santa Rosa.
California. An Episcopalian. he was a long time member of Holy Innocents
Episcopal Church in Cone Madera. and currently is a member of the
congregation of Grace Cathedral, San Francisco.
He served thirty-one years as an executive with Pacific Bell. retiring in
1988. His last position was that of District Manager in charge of Construction
and Engineering tor the East Bay. His executive and administrative experiences included Personnel, Budget. Results. and Training Development/Delivery. He also taught at the Bell laboratories in New Jersey in 1972.
He is a recognized expert in the Capital Budget Funding Process, and still
serves as a Consultant to Pacific Telesis International, and to the Raychem
Corporation of Menlo Park. California.
Our Grand Master was Raised in Marin Lodge No. 191, San Rafael, in
1968, and progressed through the Officers Corp until his installation as
Master of the lodge in 1976. He served the Marin Lodge Temple Board as
a member from 1973 - 1976. and in 1976 was the President of the 114th
Masonic District Officers Association.
His service with the Grand Lodge of California included membership on
the Lodge Resource and Development Committee. and membership on the
Board of Trustees of the California Masonic Memorial Temple. He served
the latter as President from 1983 to 1987. In 1977-78 he served as Junior
Grand Deacon for Grand Master Donald B. McCaw. He also served four
years as a trustee of the Masonic Homes of California. Elected as Junior
Grand Warden in 1987, he was successively elected as Senior Grand
Warden. Deputy Grand Master. and then elected and installed as Grand
Master of Masons in California on October 16. 1991.

STANLEY M . CAZNEAUX
Grand Master
October 16. 1991 - October 14, 1992

iii

Grand Master Cazneaux is a member of the Scottish Rite and the York
Rite. as well as Islam Temple of the Shrine. He belongs to a number of
concord;mt and appendant bodies of Masonry in California. His Masonic
career also includes having served as Associate Bethel Guardian of Bethel
No. 48. International Order of Job's Daughters. from 1968 - 1975. His civic
assodations include service on the Board of Directors of the Goodwill
Industries of Santa Clara County, including a term on the Executive Board.
He is a member of the linfield Oaks Association in Menlo Park. as well as
many other civic associations.

The 1992 Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge of California
began with an Informal Opening of Grand lodge at 2:00 p.m .. on Sunday.
October 11, 1992, to which members of the public were invited. The session
was held in the Masonic Auditorium of the California Masonic Memorial
Temple in San Francisco. and was followed by a Vesper Service at Grace
Cathedral across the street.
The program began with the entrance of the 1992 Grand Lodge Officers.
The informal session of Grand Lodge was ra ised by Worshipful Chris Leslie.
a Past Master of St. Boniface Lodge No. 8790, United Grand Lodge of
England. followed by the formal presentation of the Flag ot the United States
of America, and all those in attendance pledged their allegiance to the Flag.
This was followed with a "Tribute to the Flag" by Royal Dano. a noted
television and moving picture celebrity. as well as a Past Master of Santa
Monica-Palisades Lodge No. 307.
Grand Master Stanley M. Cazneaux then welcomed all those in attendance, and briefly explained the activities which were to come.
Visiting Grand Masters. and representatives of Grand Masters of other
Grand Lodges. were then escorted into the auditorium and introduced by
the Grand Marshal and welcomed by the Grand Master He noted that those
guests who were currently the Grand Masters ot their respective jurisdictions
would be formally welcomed with Masonic Grand Honors at the Opening of
the Annual Communication again on Monday, October 12, and that all the
visiting Grand Masters and representatives were welcome to attend the
forthcoming sessions of the Grand Lodge of California .
Those present were as follows

After introduction. the Visiting Dignitaries were escorted to seats
reserved for them in the auditorium.

The Chairman of the Lodge Resources Development Committee. Worshipful Robert Kulick, then presented the Grand Master's Achievement
Awards to the Masters of the lodges who had earned them. A complete
listing will be found in the report of that committee in Appendix J, page 356.

6

At this point. William Pherrin, Potentate of El Bekal Shrine Temple.
Anaheim. rnade a special presentation to the Grand Master of a plaque
honoring him for his service in working to enhance communication between
the Shrine and the Grand Lodge during his term of office
Grand Master Cazneaux welcomed these special guests. and expressed his appreciation for these representatives of the Family of
Freemasonry in Cal rtornia They were then also escorted to seats reserved
for them in the auditorium.
The youth leaders of the three youth orders were then escorted into the
auditorium and introduced They were:
Miss Cynthia Jepson
Miss Cheryl Saylor
Mr. Jeffrey L. Milde
Mr. Christopher Burns

. . . . . . . . . . Grand Worthy Advisor

Southern California DeMolay Association

Each in turn made brief remarks to the assembled guests in the
auditorium. and were welcomed. in turn. by Grand Master Cazneaux. The
Grand Master then presented to each of the leaders a check from the
California Masonic Found<1tion in the amount of $2,000 for the Grand Bethel,
International Order of Job's Daughters, the Grand Assembly, International
Order of Rainbow for Girls. the Northern California DeMolay Association.
and the Jurisdiction of Southern California. Order of DeMolay. These
checks are for training of youth leaders in these respective organizations
and jurisdictions.
The Chairman of the Youth Orders Committee. Worshipful David Denbow, then presented the Grand Master's Youth Achievement Awards to
Brothers Waller L. Mosier, Chula Vista Lodge No. 626 Wallace ·Al' Kelley.
Petaluma-Hamilton Lodge No. 180, John A Hernandez. Palo Alto-Roller
Lodge No. 346 and William A Peacock. Fox-Coates Daylight Lodge No. 842.
These prestigious awards are for service to all three of the youth orders
in California over a lengthy period of time, and their presentation by the
Grand Master is a mark of singular honor paid to these devoted members
of the Craft.

7

Grand Master Stanley M. Cazneaux then introduced Dr. Jim Tunney,
keynote speaker for the program. Dr. Tunney is a noted motivational
speaker, educator, and former NFL referee, who spoke on "Here's to the
Winners' " His remarks were very well received by the audience, who gave
him a standing ovation at the conclusion.
The Benediction was then delivered by the Grand Chaplain. the Very
Reverend Larry W. Coyle. and the Grand Master and Grand Lodge Officers
then recessed from the auditorium. leading the guests across the street to
Grace Cathedral for the Vesper Service.

VESPER SERVICE
The 1992 Vesper Service opened at Grace Cathedral. San Francisco. at
4:45 p.m. on Sunday. October 11, with an organ prelude by the Cathedral
Organist. and the procession of the Grand Master and Mrs. Cazneaux.
accompanied by other Grand Lodge Officers and their ladies. More than
1.200 were present. with standing room only. The Invocation was given by
the Grand Chaplain. the Very Reverend Larry W. Coyle. The Grand Bible
Bearer. E.B. "Jay· Rider gave a reading from First Corinthians Chapter 13.
Mrs Cazneaux then sang a beautiful solo ent itled. "Love." with music by May
Van Dyke.
The Grand Chaplain gave an address to those assembled. which was
followed by an organ "meditation" by the Cathedral Organist. Mrs. Cazneaux then sang "The Lord's Prayer:· by Albert Hay Malotte and the service
was concluded with a Benediction by the Grand Chaplain. A Closing Hymn,
"Joyful. Joyful. We Adore Thee," was sung. and an organ postlude by the
Cathedral Organist ended an inspirational beginning to Grand Lodge Week.

The Grand Master then asked the Grand Secretary to call the roll of

ANNUAL COMMUNICATION
The One Hundred Forty Third Annual Communication of the Most
Worshipful Grand Lodge. Free and Accepted Masons of the State of California. commenced at 9:00 a m. on Monday. October 12. 1992. in the California
Masonic Memorial Temple. located in San Francisco. California, preceded
by an organ recital by the Grand Organist. Worshipful Harold M. Hand.
The opening session of the Annual Communication was raised by
Worshipful Robert F. Lowry and Worshipful Roy W. Troutman. PM. Master
and Secretary. respectively, of Mann Lodge No. 191. They used a gavel first
presented to Most Worshipful Stanley M. Cazneaux. Grand Master of
Masons in California, when he was installed as Master of Marin Lodge No.
191 on January 10, 1976. and again presented to him when he was installed
as Grand Master in October. 1991.
Other gavels used throughout the morning session included the following:
The gavel presented to the Grand Master at the 611 th Masonic
District Reception on November 8. 1991.
The gavel presented to the Grand Master at the East Bay Masonic
Officers· Association Spring Breakfast. April 26. 1992. The gavel
was handcrafted by Worshipful Hnrold Acre. Past Master.
The gavel presented to the Grand Master at the Reception at
Hollywood Lodge No .. 355 on January 30. 1992.
The gavel presented to the Grand Master at the l 07th and 108th
Masonic Districts' Reception on November 19. 1991, and hand·
crafted by students of Petaluma High School. under the direction
of Instructor Rollin Gehring.
The Grand Master then reported to the Grand Lodge on the success of
the Informal Opening of Grand Lodge in the Masonic Auditorium the day
before. and also on the success of the Vesper Service at Grace Cathedral.
He invited those present to nttend the Grand Master's Ball scheduled for
the Hyatt Regency Hotel at s·oo pm on Tuesday. October 13.

The Most Worshipful Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted Masons of
California was opened in ample form w ith the Grand Master presiding The
Flag of the United States of America was formally received and presented
at the Altar. after which the assembled Brethren joined in the Pledge of
Allegiance to the Flag The Tribute to the Flag was then given by Royal Dano.
the sa me that he had given the day before at the Informal Opening of Grand
Lodge.
. Present during the Annual Communic ation were all 26 Grand Lodge
Officers, 19 Past Grand Masters. 2 Past Grand Officers. 834 Lodge Officers
and representa tives from 433 Lodges, 488 Past Masters. 1OB District Inspectors, 200 Grand Lodge Committee men. 132 Secretaries of Lodges (some of
whom were also Past Masters. and included in that count), 153 Master
Masons not members of Grand Lodge, and 30 visitors from other jurisdictions. of which,.9 w ere Grand Masters The total attendanc e was 2.001.

letters to the six Past Grand Masters who were unable to attend the Annual
Communic ation.

REMARKS OF BROTHER ERNEST BOAGN INE

It was moved by the Deputy Grand Ma ster. and seconded by the Grand
Lecturer. to permit the introduction of an emergent resolution. The motion
was carried una nimously. It was then moved by the Deputy Grand Master
to waive Section 200 of the California Masonic Code which requires the
Installation of Grand Lodge Officers as the last item of business of Grand
Lodge. and instead to permit the Installatio n of Officers to take place on
Wednesday, October 14. at 2:00 p.m The motion was carried unanimously.

The Grand Master then introduced Brother Ernest Borgnine. a member
of Hollywood Lodge No. 355, who briefly addressed Grand Lodge.
GRANO CHAPLAIN 'S ADDRESS
The Grand Master then called on the Grand Chaplain. the Very Reverend
L;Jrry W. Coyle, to bri ng an inspiratio nal message to Gra nd Lodge. The text
of the message, which was very well received by those present, is found in
Appendix G, page 194 of the Proceedings
MEMORIAL SERVICE
The Annual Memorial Service was then cond ucted und c:r th e direction
of the Chairman of the Memorial Service Committee, Worshipful Robert
Moore. PM. assist ed by the Very Reverend Larry W. Coyle. Grand Chaplain.
Those remembered at the service were those members of Grand Lod ge who
had died during the precedin g year, and a complete list is included in the
Report of the Memorial Committee. found in Appendix J. page 374.

RULES OF DEBATE
The Grand Master explained that he, or wh omever was presid ing, would
personally notify each speaker when the time limit for speaking had been
reached (usually three minutes for each speaker on a Resolutio n}. rather
than shutting off the microphone as in previous years. Warning lights would
be used by th~ Grand Secretary to time debate. but the use of the lights
would be for the Grand Master. or the presider. only.

The Grand Master then called on Very Worshipful Robert A. Klinger, Past
Grand Secretary. who presented the Report of the Finance Committee.
which may be found in Appendix J, page 346. The report also included a
recommendation of $14.32 for the Per Capita for the Fiscal Year Ending
October 31. 1993. including $1. 1O for the California Freemason magazine.
Before the vote on Per Capita was taken. Right Worshipful A. Stephen
Doan, Deputy Grand Master. asked permission to address the Grand Lodge.
He noted that the 4% increase in the proposed Per Capita only offsets the
loss of income due to the loss of membership since the previous Annual
Communication. He also noted that the Grand Lodge had changed the
fiscal year to match the term of the Grand Master, rendering him more
accountable for maintaining expenditures within budget. He outlined the
reductions that had been made to produce the budget in the Finance
Committee Report. The Deputy Grand Master also discussed plans for the
next Grand Lodge year and recognized the Grand Master for his service to
the Craft during the current Grand Lodge year. The text of his comments
are found in Appendix 8. page 79.
It was moved. seconded. and carried to establish a Per Capita of $14.32
for the Grand Lodge fiscal year November 1, 1992 through October 31. 1993.

BUDG ET HEROES OF 1991-1992

BUDGET RESULTS
Early in my tenure as an Elected Grand Lodge Officer. it became evident
to me that there were inherent problems within the budgeti ng process. It
culminated in 1991 , when our reserves were exhausted and we had to
borrow $100,000 in order to complete the Grand Lodge year. Some said it
was only a "cash flow'' problem . However. the problem was becoming more
acute each year. We would soon find ourselves in the same position as the
State and Federal government budgets • going broke.

33

When starting the budget process in early 1991, I wrote to the group
chairmen of the committees of our Grand Lodg e. I set the parameters for
submicting a budget request. In that correspondence. I stated that "Th~
Budget Heroes of 1991-1 992' would be those comminees t.hat met all th.eir
goals and objectives. and underran their budgets. I would like to recognize
th<>se ''Heroes at this time.

II is my feeling that the ultimate responsibility for a balanced budget lies
with the Grand Master. If that is the case, then he should be tne one who
should play a strong role in the building of that budget. Your Executive
Committee took on that task to build a budget we could manage and cont rol.
I stood before you last October, as Deputy Grand Master. to explain the
process we used to build the budget. and asked tor your approval. Once
again you gave me your full support. I am pleased to report to you that we
have completed this Grand Lodge year within the budget levels approved
by you last year. In addition, we have paid back the $100,000 borrowed in
the previous year. and still ended the year with a small surplus. In order to
accomplish this budget control. we cut every line item in the 1991-1992
budget. from Grand Master's travel expenses down through every committee of this Grand Lodge.

5.36% underrun

Included in those cuts was the operation of the Grand Lodge office. In
addition to the tightly controlled expenses that our Grand Secretary, John
L. Cooper, presented to us, we took an additional $50,000 out of the budget
and told him to make the necessary cuts to accomplish this. I am pleased
that our Grand Secretary met the budget levels we set tor the ottice
operation. It did not come without some pain. The cuts resulted in lay-offs
of two employees in the office. One such lay-ott resulted in a ' Wrongful
Termination Suit" against the Grand Lodge of California by our former
Membership Administrator. I can assure you that his lay-off was justified

GRAND LODGE PER CAPITA
In Town Hall Meetings throughout this State, I have told many of you
that I would love to be the Grand Master who would stand before you and
tell you that the Per Capita will be lowered, or at least kept at the same level.
That is simply not going to happen in the near future. As long as we have a
declining membership. and increasing costs. there will be an increase in the
Per Capita.
I can assure you that because of the steps that have been taken by your
Executive Committee to control the budget expenditures. increases over the
nex1 several years will not exceed the cost of living index. currently in the
range of 2% to 5% annually.
NOB HILL MASONIC MANAGEMENT. INC
The financial and budgetary concerns of this Grand Lodge required your
Executive Commit1ee last year to look at every possible source of revenue
which could reduce or even eliminate our continued financial decline.
Among other avenues explored we directed a study to be made of the
potential profit centers of the California Masonic Memorial Temple and found
that our beautiful auditorium and exhibit hall were severely under-utilized
We also found that profit from our on-site 450 car parking garage could
greatly be enhanced through a different method of operation in the future.
Fallowing extensive professional evaluation and legal advice by our Past
Grand Master. Most Worshipful Donald 8. McCaw. the Nob Hill Masonic
Management. Inc .. a California for-profit corporation, was formed with the
Grand Lodge of California being the sole stockholder. The new corporation
entered into a lease agreement with the California Masonic Memorial Temple
corporation whereby all of the profit-making operations of the Temple were
assumed by Nob Hill Masonic Management. with rent paid to the Temple in
amounts at least equal to its average prior annual earnings.
At present. the Nob Hill Masonic Management has retained the general
management services of Dakota Enterprises. Inc., whose principal stock·
holder and president is Ray W Ward, Certified Facilities Executive. and
former executive vice president and general manager of the Oakland,

Your Grand Lodge is looking forward. within a reasonable period of time.
to the receipt of substantial dividends from these new and expanded
operations. and will report to you on a regular b asis

PROCE EDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFOR NIA

STATE OF THE CRAFT

At this meeting , issues of mutual interest were discussed, calendars
were exchanged, and reports were given on the activities of the following
organizations:

Each of us is deeply concerned about the future of our great Fraternity.
It is my impression that we will continue to see a decline in our membership
numbers in future years. In fact, we probably may never achieve the large
membership we once had. However, I believe that the young men that are
currently joining our Fraternity are dedicated to. and active in, their Lodges.
They are dedicated to the ritual of our Grand Lodge. And most importantly,
they are dedicated to the principles of Freemasonry. With a renewed
membership, made up of young men I ike this, our Fraternity will continue to
be a strong, viable force in society, Tod ay, Tomorrow and in the Twenty-First
Century.
We must encourage the younger members of our Lodges to slay active
by allowing them to assume leadership roles and attempt new and interesting programs. In too many of our Lodges today, I see the older members
controlling the actions and activities of the Lodge. If they continue on this
course, the Lodge will surely not survive into the 21st Century. Our
forefathers left to us a great legacy in our beautiful Lodges, Masonic Homes
and the very presence of Freemasonry in the communities of our great State.
It would be regrettable if we did not leave that same legacy for our children
and our children's children
An area of concern that needs continued attention is our ritual. Our
Grand Lecturer, Ulysses G. Patropulos, along w ith the Assistant Grand
Lecturers and Inspectors of this Jurisdiction, spend endless hours teaching
the ritual of this Grand Lodge. Yet we continue to see. in many areas, a
continued deterioration in the quality of our officers work. To those of you
who do quality ritual work, I congratulate you. To those of you who need to
improve, I charge you to recommit yourselves to quality ritual. and then work
to accomplish that goal. Each of us takes great pride in the excellent ritual
work of our Grand Lodge Officers exemplified at the opening of this Grand
Lodge. Let us also take great pride in our own Lodge openings and in the
degrees we give our candidates. Our candidates and membership will
accept no less.

ROSE PARADE FLOAT
On January 1, 1992, the 103rd Tournament of Roses Parade took place
in Pasadena, California, and the "Family of Freemasonry'' was a participant
In this spectacular event. With one million people lining Colorado Boulevard
and three hundred twenty-four million people around the world watching on
television, our float, "Voyagers of Discovery" passed In review. Although rt
did not receive a trophy, it did gain the recognition of the President of the
Tournament of Roses. Mr. Robert L. Cheney, who personally placed our float
In front of his car in the parade. This allowed us to be in twenty-eighth place
In the parade, and aftorded us excellent visibility to the world -wide audience.

observed Grand Maste rs receiving gifts for attending various functions. At

students for years to come. Wilh the approval of that Board. the Stanley
and Gloria Cazneaux Scholarship for Students of the Arts' was established.

have committed to donations toward this fund over a period of four years.

GRAND MASTER'S DECISIONS

40

COMMITIEES OF GRAND LODGE
The Committees of this Grand Lodge have long been a concern of mine.
Some have been very successful, while others seem to accomplish nothing
To address this problem . your Executive Committee allempted to meet with
as many committee chairmen as possible during 1991 1992. The objective
of these meetings was to share concerns and problems. to set goals and
objectives. and to provide training where needed. These meetings were a
resounding success.
One of the most common problems confronting a Grand Master.
generally has been in the area of Temple Properties. I was determined to
eliminate that time consuming task during my year as Grand Master. In an
Executive Committee meeting in Fresno we met with all the members of the
Temple Properties Committee. We set specific goals and objectives for the
year and A. Stephen Doan, our Deputy Grand Master. conducted a three
hour training session for all in attendance. The results of this meeting were
excellent. During my term as Grand Master only one major issue on Temple
Properties ever reached my desk for action. I want to recogni2e and thank
C. Richard Hutchinson and his Temple Properties Committee for their
outstanding job during this Masonic Year.
The Executive Committee has met w ith at least one or two committee
chairmen at each of our monthly meeti ngs. These meetings have resulted
in more eftective committees and rnore cost ettective operations of those
committees. I want to thank each of those Chairmen for their time. ettorts
and dedication to this Grand Lodge.
There is more work to be done. I know that our Deputy Grand Master
and his 1992-1993 Executive Committee are committed to continuing this
process. Your Grand Lodge committees stand ready to assist each and
every Lodge in this jurisdiction, whenever called upon. Use the expertise
that is available to you through these dedicated Masons who give so
generously of their time to serve you.

41

.Gfaod Master's Decision No. 1 - Removal of Master of Foothill Lodge No
~

Shortly after my installation as your Grand Master. I met with Robert B.
Eckstein. who was then serving as Master of Foothill Lodge No 564 and
with Donald Scha uer, Jr .. the then Secretary of that Lodge The purpose of
that meeting was to discuss the contents, preparation of and mailing. prior
to the 1991 Annual Communication of this Grand Lodge, ot a letter advocat ing the election of a specified Brother as Grand Secreta ry
Although the subject letter was not signed by Brothers Eckstein and
Schauer. but was instead signed by the wives of each of these brethren, it
was admitted to me by the said brethren that the letter was thus prepared
and signed as a result of discussions had by the brethren and their wives
after the Inspector of the District had specifically told the brethren that it they
were to author and mail such a letter. it would constitute a violation of Section
2074 of our California Masonic Code and subject them to charges of
Unmasonic Conduct. They admitted, further. that copies of the letter were
mailed to addresses obtained from the copyrighted Roster of this Grand
Lodge as furnished to the Lodge with postage provided through the use of
the non-profit 01ailing permit obtained for official Lodge use.
Based upon my findings arising. in the main, from the brethren's own
admissions to me and based. further, upon their total lack of remorse. I
decided that. in furtherance of my duty as Grand Master to exercise a careful
supervision over the Craft and to see that our law was strictly obeyed. that
good reasons existed for disciplinary action Specifically, I found that each
of the said brethren had been negligent in the proper performance of his
respective duties in that:

1 By permitting the non -profit mailing permit. issued by the United
States Postal Service to the Lodge for Lodge business use, to be used for
the mailing of the subject "campaign·• letter constituted either a violation of
Federal Law. if the letter was to be considered as "non·Lodge business" or
a violation of CMC Section 2074. if it was considered to be proper Lodge
business.
2. Having been advised by the Inspector of the District that the
distribution of such a letter by a Mason would constitute a Masonic offense
under CMC Section 3308. the discussion of the matter by the brethren with

43

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

their wives and the ultimate preparation of the letter to be signed by the wives
only could eas~y be defined as participation in a conspiracy on the
brethren's part to evade the law.

GRAND MASTER'S RECOMMENDATIONS
fiitCommendation No 1 ·Amendments to New California Masonic Code

3. The failure on the part of the said Master and Secretary to protect
the confidentiality of the Roster of this Grand Lodge and to properly
safeguard the use of the Lodge's non-profit mailing permit was illustrative
of a neglect of duty.

At the 1991 Annual Communication. the Special Committee on Code
Revision presented for adoption by this Grand Lodge. the revised California
Masonic Code which it had been preparing since its appointment following
the 1988 Annual Communication

In a spirit of Masonic benevolence, I issued an Edict directed to Brother
Eckstein setting forth a series of conditions which. if met. would save him
from being suspended as Master of his Lodge. Basically. a letter of apology
was to be prepared. signed, and submitted to me for approval prior to
November 30, 1991. Said letter was thereafter to be mailed. at no expense
to the Lodge, to each Lodge in this jurisdiction prior to December 7. 1991
and Brother Eckstein was to apologize to his Lodge at its December Stated
Meeting. Said Master was also instructed to remove Brother Schauer as
Lodge Secretary and to satisfy certain other conditions Brother Eckstein
was specifically advised that a failure on his part to satisfy all of the conclitions
and to report his compliance to me prior to December 10. 1991. would result
in his immediate suspension as Master of Foothill Lodge.

Pursuant to the request of the Special Committee. this Grand Lodge
adopted the new Code but provided that it would become effective following
this, the 1992 Annual Communication.

42

Brother Eckstein did remove Brother Schauer from his duties as
Secretary of Foothill Lodge. and did make an honest attempt to apologize
to his Lodge at the December Stated Meeting. Unfortunately. Brother
Eckstein chose to defy his Grand Master and to ignore a major ponion of
the Edict. No letter of apology was ever prepared nor submitted to me as
required.
Failure of Brother Eckstein to submit the required letter of apology to
me by the November 30. 1991 date made it impossible for him to comply
with all of the conditions of the Edict. Atter numerous attempts by the District
Inspector to open up a line of communication between Brother Eckstein and
the Grand Master, Brother Eckstein still refused to write or make a phone
call to me
Regrettably, because of his non-compliance with the terms of my Edict.
therefore. and for good reasons shown. I notified Brother Eckstein on
December 6, 1991, that he stood suspended as Master of Foothill Lodge
No. 564 effective that date

The intervening year was to provide an opportunity for other committees
to review the new Code and to point out any apparent errors or omissions.
Such a review has been completed and a number of modifications have
been recommended to me to clarify language or to incorporate in the new
Code matters which appeared in the 1975 Code but were inadvertently
omitted from the teXl of the new Code.

I am satisfied that the text of these modifications do not constitute or
contain any substantive changes in our law but are in the nature of
"housekeeping.matters" which will make a superb work product even better.
Accordingly. I recommend the adoption of the various amendments to
the California Masonic Code (1991 edition) as set forth in detail in the
Appendix to this message and which were previously distributed by the
Grand Secretary.
Recommendation No. 2 · Discontinue CommittC'es on Masonic Code and
Review of Proceedings as Standino Committees of Grand Locl1;1e
As the membership of this Grand Lodge declines and our cost of
operating increases. it is incumbent upon us to actively seek to increase
efficiency and to eliminate duplication so as to result in the best value for
the dollars expended .
In this regard, it is important that we use the experience gained from
past years of operation to examine the committee structure of this Grand
lodge and eliminate those committees whose functions we can do without
or whose functions can be performed by another existing committee.

44

PROCEEDINGS OF TH E

Since the Grand Secretar y's office is, by the very nature of its operation .
constantly in touch with the other Grand Lodges of the world and is kept
informed of the events occurring in those Grand Jurisdict ions, it is my
opinion that the existence of a separate committe e to duplicate this work is
a luxury that we can no longer afford.
Likewise. the work ot the Masonic Code Committee would appear. to a
great extent. to duplicate work already performed by the Committ ee on
Jurisprudence and in those areas wherein there is no duplicati on. the
assignment of those few tasks to the Committ ee on Jurisprudence would
not. in any way, overburd en that Committee.
Accordingly, I recomme nd elimination of the Committees on Review ot
Proceedings and on the Masonic Code as standing committe es of this
Grand Lodge.
Recomm endation No. 3 - Amend CMC to Modify Title. Size. and Duties of
Commin ee on Tempie Prooerties
Grand Master Sherod, during his tenure as my predecessor. decided to
conduct an experiment and assign the work of the Committ ee on Taxation
to the Committ ee on Temple Properties. During my year as your Grand
Master. I continue d that experiment and I am satisfied that this is a proper
committee to be assigned both functions since. to a great extont, the
problems of taxation deal with the operation s of our Temple Associations
and the Temples that they maintain.
I am satisfied, also. however. that the committe e needs an increase in
the number of member s in order to provide necessar y advice and counsel
to the Lodges of this jurisdicti on as they exist from border to border in this
giant State.
Accordingly, I recomme nd that our law be amended to change the title
ot the Comminee on Temple Properties to Temple Properties ancl Taxation.
and to increase the size of that committe e to 15 members.
Recomm endation No. 4 - Amend CMC to Beauire Three ·Black Balls' for
Rejection of a Candida te
It is my belief that all too otten. potential ly good applican ts are being
rejected by virtue of a black ball vote cast by one biased. angry or
unreasonable member who has permitted his personal feelings to interfere
with a dispassionate and realistic consideration of the contribut ions that the
rejected applican t could make to Masonry On several occasion s this year

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

45

1 have been notified by Brothers of this Grand Lodge ot the rejection of

candidates because of obvious religious or racial reasons. However. the
most serious incident occured when a Brother hid behind the ballot box and
rejected a total of five (5) candidates at two consec utive Stated Meetings
just to get at the officers of the Lodge. One of those candidates was a
member of the Crty Council. It is my thought. therefore. that this problem
could be solved by eliminating this ·one man control and requiring the
negative vote to reject a candidate by a realistic three or more 'black balls'
or by three or more members filing a 'person to person" objection to the
Master of the Lodge prior to the day or the vote
Accordin gly, it is my recomme ndation that our California Masonic Code
(1991 edition) be amended to provide that three or more brethren must cast
"Black Cubes" at the time of the ballot. or object in person to the Master
before ballot, to constitut e rejection of an applicant.
SHAINE MEMORANDUM OF UNDERSTANDING
In a spirit of cooperation and with the hope of creating a situation
benefiting both the Grand Lodge of California and our brethren holding
membership in the various Shrine Temples in this jurisdicti on. your Executive Committee met in October of 1991 to draft a Memorandum of Understanding" to be presented to the seven Potentates of the Shrine Temples of
California . That 'draft Memoran dum was then taken to a special meeting of
our Past Grand Masters in November. seeking their advice and counsel. and
their suppon. After a long and difficult meeting, we had the unanimo us
support of every Past Grand Master present. I t hen went to Tampa. Florida
and presented the Memora ndum to John W. Dean Ill, Imperial Potentate of
North America He also gave his full support to this Memofan dum.

I then contacte d the Potentates and Chief Rabbans of each of the said
Shrine Temples and proposed that each of them agree on behalf of their
Temple that:
1. It would not solicit. sponsor, insert, place or print advertising of
any kind in or for any medium or publicatio n which purports to be
Masonic or is otherwise associated with the Shrine. in name or
character. or which is published by. or for the Shrine. or for any
other body, order or organization affiliated with the Shrine or otherwise requiring Masonic affiliation as a condition to membership.
2. Use of all such advertising previously solicited would cease within
sixty (60) days following the date of our agreement .

agreed to grant. pursuant to the authority contained in Sections 2062 and

sponsorship by that Shrine Temple of any financial undertaking and commercial venture. whether for profit or not, and solicitation by the Temple of

The Warden's Weekend Management Retreats continue to be a huge
success and one of the main reasons that we are seeing new and innovative
programs in our Lodges. We are also seeing significant improvement in the
quality of events. which is attracting our membership back to the Lodges.
We are truly Renewing Interest and Seeking Excellence.
I believe that the continuation of these Retreats is essential to the
revitalization of our great Fraternity in California. At the present we are only
reaching 200 of 497 Lodges in the State. We must find ways to involve the
Lodges that are not partic ipating at this time. I strongly urge all the Lodges
in this State to send their Wardens to these Retreats. I would also encourage
the Lodges to send the Warden's wives to these Management Retreats. as
they have proven to be a very important part of these training sessions.
There are separate as well as joint sessions provided for the ladies. It has
been determined by this Grand Master, and supported by the Executive
Cotnminee. that the expenses of ladies is a proper Lodge expenditure.

The success of our Retreats is spreading to other Grand Lodges in the
Western Conference. Currently Oregon, Washington and Alaska are using
the format of our Retreats to train their Wardens. They are also experiencing
a Renewed Interest in their Lodges due to the training being provided. We
wish them the same success that we have experienced here in California.

events with increased partic ipation by your membership.
ADMINISTRATIVE SEMINARS FOR INSPECTORS
Our Inspectors walk a fine line between the instruction of the ritual o f
this Grand Lodge, and the responsibility for Lodge administration. They are
responsible to the Grand Lecturer for ritual and to the Grand Master for
administration. Our Grand Lecturer has always done an excellent job of
training his people. However. the Inspectors have received little or no
training from the Grand Master. Our Past Grand Master. Ronald A. Sherod,
set-up a special committee. Inspectors' Orientation, under the leadership of
Paul T. Hodiak. His charge was to identify the needs for Inspector training.
Brother Hodiak completed his investigation and provided this Grand Master
with a detailed list of subjects requiring training. I was committed and
determined to provide that training.
This year we held seminars in each of the six Divisions following the
Ritual Committee Conference. Saturday night was given to the Deputy
Grand Master to discuss his plans and goals for his year as Grand Master.
This was an excellent opportunity for the Inspectors to have a better
understanding of the person who was to lead this Fraternity in the future. It
also gave them a clea r picture of the continuity being built into our Grand
Lodge programs.
Sunday morning gave the Inspectors the opportunity to hear from the
Grand Secretary . This was followed by formal presentations on Temple
Properties, Protocol, Insurance. and Laws relating to the use of alcoholic
beverages in Lodges. The day was concluded with remarks from the Grand
Master.
This was an excellent start on a much needed program. There are many
more subjects to cover in this type of training session. Once again. your
Executive Committee is committed to the continuation of this important
program.

49

THE UNITED GRAND LODGE OF ENGLAND
It was my honor and privilege to represent the 140,000 Masons of this
jurisdiction at the 180th Anniversary of t he Grand Lodge of Greece. and the
275th Anniversary of the United Grand Lodge of England.
The Grand Lodge of Greece met in Athens on May 14-20. 1992. This
Anniversary Celebration was well attended by Grand Masters from around
the world. It was my pleasure to have the opportunity to address this Grand
Lodge session. and bring greetings from the members of the Grand Lodge
of California. The hospitality of our Greek Brothers was outstanding. They
provided a sight-seeing tour of Athens. and a one day cruise through the
Greek Islands. Gloria and I were fortunate to have Worshipful Michael
Balopoulos, Grand Tiler of the Grand Lodge of California, attend the Grand
Lodge sessions with us. His assistance throughout our stay in Athens made
the trip that much more enjoyable. Jt was an experience that will long live
in our memories.
The United Grand Lodge of England met in London on June 9th, 10th
and 11th. 1992. to celebrate its 275th Anniversary. It was also the 25th
Anniversary of the Installation of HRH The Duke of Kent. as Grand Master.
Because of the large number of people attend ing, over 12.000. the meeting
was held in Earls Court. and on this special occasion ladies and the press
were invited to attend. To my amazement the Grand Master call ed the Grand
Lodge from Refreshment to Labor. and conducted the business of the Grand
Lodge. with the television cameras rolling and the lad ies in attendance. It
was a historic event in Freemasonry.
There were eighty-four Grand Lodges represented at this Anniversary
Celebration, sixty-four of whom were Grand Masters. It was my rare
privilege to be escorted to the East and to be greeted by the Grand Master.
HRH The Duke of Kent. l will always cherish the memory of the moment.
In addition to the Anniversary Celebration. I had the opportunity to
participate in a one day conference at Freemasons' Hall The theme of the
conference was ·Freemasonry - Today, Tomorrow. 2000.'' The Grand
Master asked each of his Provincial Grand Masters to have two young
Master Masons in attendance to share the viewpoint of the younger Masons
in England. The conference provided a forum for open and honest communication. and was a benefit to all in attendance.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

This was a "once-in-a-lifetime" experience tor this Grand Master. I want
the members of this Grand Lodge to know how much Gloria and I appreciate
the opportunity to represent each of you at this most prestigious event

ACKNOWLEDGMENTS
As this Grand Lodge Year draws to a close. I want to extend my sincere
thanks to the Elected and Appointed Officers who have served this Grand
Lodge so very well during 1991-1992. They have traveled up and down this
great State, anending the scheduled events on almost every weekend . They
have traveled to towns they did not know e)(isted. They took advantage of
these visits to meet and greet the Brethren of the constituent Lodges. They
listened to what these Brothers had to say, and shared thoughts and ideas
that could make our Lodges stronger. These officers and their ladies have
served you in the very finest manner possible. I am proud to have had the
privilege to serve with each of them.
I want to extend a very special thank you to our Deputy Grand Master.
R. Stephen Doan. and his wife Donna Over the past three years we have
had the opportunity to become very close friends. Steve has given me
excellent advice and counsel that has helped make this a successful
Masonic Year.
The office of Grand Master is one of enormous responsibilities and no
Grand Master can be successful without the support of many people. I
would like to acknowledge the support of our Grand Secretary. John L.
Cooper and his outstanding staff, Terry Hobson and his Masonic Memorial
Temple staff. the Assistant Grand Lecturers and Inspectors, our Board
members and Commineemen, the Masters and Wardens of the Lodges and
all the Brethren of this jurisdiction. You have made this a very enjoyable
experience for this Grand Master.
This experience in Freemasonry would not have been possible for me
without the support of my wife. Gloria. She has always been there to help
me as I accepted additional responsibilit ies in Lodge and Grand Lodge. As
we celebrated our 37th Wedding Anniversary at Live Oak Lodge No 6 t . we
were reminded by the Brethren of that Lodge that ours had been a · Lifetime
of Loving, Sharing and Caring.' Gloria has shared those same attributes
with the members of this Grand Lodge. She has represented you with great
dignity wherever she has traveled. I thank her for all the contributions she
has made during this Masonic Year

51

One year ago, I knelt at the altar to take the obligation of the highest
office in Freemasonry. At that moment. I realized the awesome respon ·
sibility that I was about to assume. and turned to the Great Architect ot the
Universe for help. I asked ·
Dear Father -· from whom comes every good and perfect gift. and the
source of all our light and direction, will you be pleased to grant unto me,
the vision to see beyond today and to effect ively plan for tomorrow. Give
me the strength and courage to do those things that need to be done. May
my every action reflect the brotherly love that binds and cements us
together. May all things be done in a sense of oneness. that will llft our
beloved Fraternity to new heights. May my every decision be that which will
further the spreading of the light of Masonry.
My Brethren. you have given me the opportunity to serve this great
Fraternity. and the Great Architect of the Universe gave me the strength and
wisdom to accomplish this Masonic Year. Each of you must judge for
yourselves whether I have been successful I sincerely and deeply appreciate the love and support you have given to me. As I have traveled
throughout our State this year. I have left the Brethren with this final thought:
A bell is not a bell until someone rings it.
A song is not a song until someone sings it.
Brother Love is not Brotherly Love until YOU practice it
And Freemasonry is not Freemasonry until YOU live it
I charge each and every one of you to:
Ring the bell!
Sing the song!
Practice Brotherly love'
And live Freemasonry'
Thank you. God Bless each and every one of you.

81

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

The other anticipated members of next year's Executive Committee are
commirted to a balanced Grand Lodge budget. elimination of unnecessary
Grand Lodge expenditures and a limit on per capita increases to the cost of
living. If you give us that cost of living increase each year. even though the
gross amount raised may remain level or eventually decline. we will do our
best to give you the best Grand Lodge programs possible for the money
spent

Mason in a Lodge will take the responsib1l1ty to see it happen. That one man
will spark others. They will have Lodge programs that their members want
The members will want to come to Lodge because their Lodge offers
fellowship and service to others. Most imponantly. Lodge w~ I offer the
opportunity to reflect on the principles of Masonry which set us apart from
other secular groups and help us answer the concerns which confront us.
These are the keys to a successful Lodge. This is how Freemasonry will

80

rebuild.
Obviously. over time. some programs must be eliminated. A fraternity
of 130.000 today cannot have the same programs that it did twenty years
ago with almost twice the membership. Just give us the same amount per
member. adjusted for inflation, and we will do what we can, for as long as
we can. to provide quality programs on that amount ot money.
Plans for Next Grand Lodge Year
The theme for the next Grand Lodge year is: "Masonry for the Twenty.
first Century" This theme challenges us to stop and consider the future of
our craft as we approach a new century and millennium. What answers does
Freemasonry have for the concerns of today? Our fraternity will only rebuild
if it can provide us a way to resolve our concerns.
Masonry is not a religion . It is not about our life in the hereafter It is
about our l~e now What do our Masonic teachings say about how we deal
with others? How we respond to crises? How we preserve our freedoms
in these times of challenge? I will explore these themes this next year in my
talks when I visit your area.
Where do we start to implement this theme? With Grand Lodge We
must continue to improve the services that Grand Lodge brings to you and
bring those services in the most cost effective manner possible. Are our
committees doing their jobs in the most effective manner? The Executive
Committee continues to review each Grand Lodge committee's performance and consider the most productive, yet cost ettective role for each in
the future. Is the Grand Lodge office doing its job in the most effective
manner? The Grand Secretary together with the members of the Executive
Committee have underway a reorganization of the Grand Lodge office. This
year, we will have a new Code Revision Committee to study whether all
duties required of the ottice by the California Masonic Code are necessary
Most importantly: the future of Freemasonry depends upon leadership
and that means local Lodge leadership Masonry will survive and rebuild
not because of any one or more Grand Masters or Grand Lodge as a whole
or its Committees or Inspectors. It will survive and rebuild because one

Grand Lodge will support these efforts through our Wardens Retreats.
OHicers Administrative Seminars. Masonic Education programs and courses from our Masonic Education Committee, training videos and tapes from
our new Video Training Committee. We will give you ideas but the real work
starts with you.
Appreciation to our Grand Master
I have worked more closely with Stan in the past three years than any
other man in this room. We have become close friends We talk often I
know Stan very well, as he knows me I have therefore gained a special
appreciation for our fraternity's great fortune in having Stan as our Grand
Master: his business background and commitment to principle have set a
standard of exc;ellence for those who may follow
Junior Wardens Retreats · Stan was th e first one to have them . The
format used today for the Junior Wardens retreats is largely the one
devised by Stan.
Budget reforms: The Grand Master is the only man with the power
to control expenses and now he is responsible for preparing the
budget which he must administer Stan changed the Grand Lodge
fiscal year to the Grand Master"s term to increase accountability of
the Grand Master.
Spending no more than the per capita collected on July 1, 1991 .
Special pilot program agreement with the Shrine on commercialism.
Willingness to look at other new ideas. such as his recommendation
at this Communication to require three cubes for rejection of an
applicant and his reforms of the Grand Lodge committee system

82

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

His steady hand at this Annual Communication: he has dealt fairly
in all things and always with brotherly love and truth as his guiding
principles.

REMARKS AFTER IN STALLATIO N

83

The theme for this new Grand Lodge year is · Masonry for the Twenty-first
Century. It challenges us to explore why we are Masons today. If we know
why we are Masons. what Masonry has to offer. then we can interest others
in joining us.

Not hing material can ever compensate a man for the sacrifices which
he makes to serve as you r Grand Master. And no man should ever aspire
to be Grand Master if he even dreams that material rewards can compensate
him. A Grand Master's reward? It is the bened iction of a job well done. the
sincere appreciation and respect from the men whom he has led

Why would someone want to be a Mason today? People join a group
normally because someone whom they already know and respect is a
member But. with intense competition for our time. that group must offer
something extra that the prospective member needs.

Let us give evidence of that appreciation and respect for our Grand
Master by a battery of three times three.

What do we need? Every thoughtful human being has concerns: need
for companionship, concerns for others. the search for the proper relationship with his God and fellow man.

If you will please rise. Together. Brethren.

What answers does Freemasonry have for the concerns of today? Our
fraternity will only rebuild if it can provide us a way to resolve our concerns.
What are our concerns? Certainly. the contemplation of the immortal
is common to all thoughtful people but that is not the purpose of Masonry.
Masonry is not·a reli gion. Religi on deals with salvation. the preparation
of our spirit for its return to the God who gave it. Masonry, on the other
hand. is about ethics: right and wrong conduct in the here and now.
Confusion sometimes arises because religion talks about ethics too but its
focus is different. Salvation makes religion and not ethics with which
Masonry deals exclusively.
Masonry is also more focused than religion Religion deals with both
salvation and ethics but Masonry with ethics alone. Yet. while more focused
than religion, Masonry is broader in scope in attracting adherents. Chris·
tianity, Judaism and Islam require a number of firmly held beliefs about God
in order to be a member. For example. most Christian Churches require a
belief in the Trinity, the resurrection and the ascension for membership.
Masonry is simpler: it requires only a belief in God and therefore peoples
of any faith who have this one simple belief may belong .

- -----

Masonry thus unites good people regardless of backgrounds or beliefs.
Masonry is unique because it is the only place where people of God may
share their search for the ethical approach to all that they do. It is the only
place where people from different religious backgrounds ca n share each's
perspective with respect to ethical behavior It is the only place where a
Christian, a Jew. a Moslem or any other believer in God may share his

84

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

approach to that same ethical conduct to which we all strive and which is
common to every major religion: doing to others as we would want them
to do to us.
These are the concerns which Masonry answers. It is not ou r concern
with salvation but our concern with now. How should we act toward our
fellow creatures? What is our mission on this earth? What standards of
conduct should we expect of ourselves, regardless of the bounds of
propriety established by the laws of our community or the expectations of
our fellow citizens. To what greater expectation of conduct does
Freemasonry call us?
Thoug htful people, humane people, caring people, the kind of people
whom we want in our fraternity. are concerned about these issues more
today than at any other time in our recent history. If we can tell them what
answers Masonry has to these concerns. our membership problems will
solve themselves. As with the proverbial man who builds the better mouse
trap, the world will beat a path to our preparation room doors.
Join me this year as we explore Masonry, its standard of ethics. what
conduct those standards require of us and how their fulfillment may answer
our concerns. the concerns of all good people, about life in the Twenty-first
Century.

The Board of Trustees
Masonic Homes of California:
We have audited the accompanying balance sheet of Masonic Homes of California (a
California nonprofit corporatio n) as of June 30, 1992 and the related statements of support,
revenue and expenses and changes in fund halances and cash Oows for the year then ende d.
These financial statementS are the responsibility of Masonic Homes' manageme nt. Our
responsibility is to express an opinion on these financial statementS based on our audit.
We conducted our audit in accordance with generally accepted auditing standards. Those
standards require that we plan and perform the audit to ohtain reasonable assurance about
whether the financial statements are free of material misstatement. An audit includes
examining. on a test basis, evidence supporting the amounts and disclosures in the financial
statements. An audit also includes assessing the accounting principles used and significant
estimates made by managemem, as well as evaluating the overall financial statement
presentation. We believe that our audit pro"ides a reasonabl e basis for our opinion.
In ou_r opinion, such financial stat~men~s present fairlv, in all material respects, the fina~cial
pos1tJon of Masonic Homes of Cahfum1a as of June 30, J992 and the results of its operations
and changes in fund balances and iu cash Oows for the year then ended in conformity with
generally ac~·epted accounting principles.

.

N discussed in Sote 4 to the financial statements, in 1992 Masorric Homes changed its
method of accounting for assigned assets and the obligation to provide future sen,ices and
the use of facilities to confonn with American Institute of Cenified Public Accountants'
Statement of Position 90-8 "Financial Accounting and Reporting by C.ominuing Care
Retirement Communities" (SOP 90-8) and, retroactively, restated the 1991 financial
statements for these changes.
Our audit was conducted for the purpose of fanning an opinion on the basic linancial
statements taken as a whole. The supplemental schedules appearing on pages 10 through 12
are presented for the purpose of additional analysis and are not a required part of the hasic
financial statemems. The supplemental schedules are t.he responsibility of Masonic H o mes'
management. Such 1992 supplemental schedules have been subjecte d to the auditing
procedures applied in the audit of the basic financial statements and in our opinion, are fairly
stated in all material respectS when considered in relation to the basic financial statements
taken as a whole.

Orianjzation • Masonic H ome-s of C'.alifornia (Masonic Homes) is a nonprofit C'.alifornia
Corporation administerin~ a home fur adults at Union City, California and a home for
adults a nd ch ildren at CoVUla, C'.alifornia. The homes are supportro by The Grand Lodge
of Free and Accepted Masons of the Staie of California (Grand Lodge) and members of
the Masonic Fratemiiy in California.
The adult residents who enter Masonic Homes arc required by a life care agreement to
assign all their assets to Masonic Homes in exchange for continuing ca re for the
remainder of the resident's life.
fund Accounting· To insu re observance of limitations and restrictions placed on the use
of resources available to Masonic Hornes, the accounts of Masonic H omes are
maintained in accordance with the principles of fund accounting.
The asseu. liabilities and fund balances of Masonic Ho mes are reported in four self·
balancing funds as follows;

e ral Fund • Resources are available to supp<Jrt the operations of the homes at
nion City and Covina, California.
Permanent Endowment Fund • Ordinances of the Grand Lodge pro.,,ide that no
portion of the principal of the Permanent Endowment Fund shall be expended for any
purpose and income from the principal shall be used only for the maintenance and
general operation of Masonic Ho mes.
Hui o M. Lorbe r Masonic Home. Union City Fund • This fund provides for the
operation, preservatio n and betterment of the home at Union City. Cillifo mia.

.

Restricted Trust Funds include bequests made for various restricted purposes.
Investments in marketable securities are stated a t fair market value and, to the extent 1hat
mana ge ment intends to hold such investments for more than one year, a rc classified as
noncurrent asscu. Gains or losses that result from market fluctuations are recognized in
the period such Ductuations occur. Realized ~a1ns or losses resulting from sales or
maturities of securities are ca lculated on an adjusted cost basis. Adjusted cost is the
market value of the sec urity at the beginning of the year. or the cost if purchased during
the year.
Propenv and equifiment are carried at cost and are depreciated on the suaight·line
method over the folowing estimated Jives:
Buildings and improvements
Equipment
Furniture and fixtures
Vehicles

received. Bequests are ge nerally contingent on the occurTence of future events and are

We have audited the acco mpanying balance shee.ts of the c.alifornia Masoni c Memorial
Temple (a California nonprofit corporation) as of June 30, 1992 and 1991 , and the related
statements of support, revenue and CXf><:nsc:s and changes in fund balances and cash flowi. for
the years then ended. These financial state-menu are the responsibility of the Temple's
management. Ou1 rc:spons ibility is to expres.s an opinion on these financial statements based
on our audits.

9

We conducted o ur aud1t5 in accordance v.i th ge ne rally acapted auditing standards. Those
standards require that we plan and perfom1 the audit to obtain reaso nable assurance about
whether the financial statements are free of ma1erial misstatement. An audit includes
examining, on a test biuis, evidence supporting the amounts and disclosures in the financial
statements. An audit also includes assessing the accounting principles used and significant
estimates made by management. as well as evaluating the overall fiDancial statement
presentation. We ~Jievc that o ur audits provide a reasonable basis for o ur o pinio n.

In our opinion, such financial statements presen1 fa trly , in all matenal respects, the financia l
position of the California Maso nic Memonal Temple as of June 30, 1992 and 1991. and the
results or its operanons and changes in fund balances and its cash nows for the years then
ended in conformity with generally accepted accounting principles.

Oricaoizatjon • The California Masonic Memorial Temple (the Temple) is a nonprofit
organi2at.ion. Income from the Temple, Jess operating expenses and reserves, 1s to be
transferred to either or both the Endowment Fund of Masonic Home..s of California
(Masoni<: Homes) and 1.h e C'.alifornia Masonic Foundation (the Foundation). The.amount and proportions of such income to be distributed is at the sole di~cretion of the
Boa.rd of Trusrees. On May 1, 19'J2, the Temple (lessor) and Nob Hill Masonic
Mfln. a. gement, Inc. (lessee) entered into. an operating lease, expiring June 30, 1997, of the
Temple auditorium, exhibit hall, parking garage and various rental properties. (See
Note 4.)
Investments in marketable securities are stated at fair market values and to the extent
that the management intends to hold such investments for more than one year, are
classified as noncurrcnt assets. Gains or losses that result from market nuctuations are
recognized in the period such fluctuations occur. Realized gains or losses resulting from
sales, or maturitie.\ of securities are calculated based on an adjusted cost basis. Adjusted
cost is the market value of the security at the bcginmng of the year, or cost if purchased
during the year.

Under the terms of the lease. Nob Hill Masonic Management, Inc. is required to pay rent
equal 10 ninety percent of the gross rental revenues. in excess of $860,000 per year until
the Jessee has paid $240,000, after which the Jessee shall pay one third of the remaining
gross rental revenues.
For the period from May I, 1992 to June 30, 1992, adjusted gross rental revenues, as
defined in the lease agreement, were not sufficient to generate any lease payments.
The financial stateroenis are not necessarily indicative of financial position and results of
operations had the Temple operated without its affiliation with Nob Hill Masonic
Management lnc.

FEDERAL EXCISE TAXES
In accordance with applicable tax provisions, the Temple is classified as a private
foundation and is subject to an e.xcisc- tax on net investment income, including realized
gains. Accordingly. federal excise taices have heen recognized in amounts of SlS,684 and
Sl 1,975 during 1992 and 1991, respectively.

RELATED PARTIES
The Grand Lodge provides general and administrative support to the Temple for which it
is reimbursed through an allocation of certain expenses. The allocation for the year
ended June 30, 1992 was $25,933.

The Temple provided office space to the Grand Lodge at $26,500 per year, until May I,
1992 when Nob Hill Masonic Management lnc. took over the lease.
Cash receipts for the Temple are received by the Grand Lodge and, on a monthly be.sis,
are transferred to the Temple.

The Board of Trustees
California Masonic Foundation:
We have audited the accompanying balance sheet of the California Masonic Foundation (a
California nonprofit corporation) as of June 30. 1992, and the related statementS of suppon,
revenue and expenses and changes in fund balances and cash flows for the year then ended.
These financial statemems are the responsibiliry of the F o undation's management. Our
responsibiliry is to express an opinion on these financial statements based on our audit.
We conducted our audi1 in accordance with general!y accepted auditing standards. Those
standards require that we plan and perform the audit to obtain reasonable assurance about
whether the financial statements are free of material misstatement. An audi1 includes
examining, on a test basis, evidence supporting the amounts and disclosures in the financial
statements. An audit also includes assessing the accounting principles used and significant
estimates made by management, as well as evaluating the overall financial statement
presentation. We believe t~at our audit pro\ides a reasonable basis for our opinion.
In our opinion, such financial statements present fairly. in all material respeclS, the financial
position of the California Masonic Foundation as of June 30, 1992, and the results of i.ls
operations and changes in fund balances and its cash Oows for the year then ended in
conformity with generally accepted accounting principles.
Our audit was conducted for the purpose of forming an opinion on the basic financial
statements taken as a whole. The supplemental s1:hc:dules appearing on pages 8 t.l uough 11
are presented for the purpose of addi11onal analysis and are not a required part of the hasic
financial statements. The supplemental schedules are the responsibility of the Foundation's
management. Such 1992 supplemental schedules have been subjected to the auditing
procedures applied i.n the audit of the basic financial statements and in our opinion, are fairly
stated in all material respects when c.onsidered in relation to the basic financial statements
taken as a whole.

ORGANIZATION AND SUMMARY OF

Oriianizalion ·The California Masonic Foundation (the Foundation) was established in
1968 by the- Grand Lodge of Free and Accepted Ma~ons of tht: State of California (Grand
Lodge). The Foundation is formed as a nonprofit charitable corporation to further all
pha~es of education, charity and science.

of resources available to the Foundation, the accounts of the Foundation are maintained
in accordance v.ith the principles of fund accounting.

funds as follow:

APPENDIX D

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

143

REPORT OF THE GRAND SECRETARY
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

My Brethren:
It is my pleasure to greet you as you attend the 143rd Annual Communication of this Grand Lodge. We have come a long way since 1650 when
our Grand Lodge was formed. We have seen many triumphs for our Grand
Lodge, including the building of the beautiful California Masonic Memorial
Temple, and the founding of many new lodges throughout the State since
1850. We have also seen the demise of many Iodges over the years, and,
since 1965, a continuing decline in the membership of our Great Fraternity
in California. We now have a membership of 133,942. This is a net loss of
5,548 members. Some were by death. some by resignation, and some by
expulsion, mostly for non-payment of dues. But all will be missed'
The continued drop in membership has required the Grand Lodge Office
this year to lay off some employees for the first time in many years. Lay offs
are never easy, but if we are to keep our costs under control they are
sometimes necessary. The entire staff of the Grand Lodge Office has
shouldered the extra burden required by less staff, and they have stepped
forward to do tlaie job with determination and fortitude. I firmly believe that
the lodges in this jurisdiction have not suffered any decline in services as a
result of our "belt tightening'' at the Grand Lodge Office. On the contrary.
those remaining have redoubled their efforts to be of service to all the
Brethren in California.
Even with a much diminished budget this year, we were still able to
rebuild the Membership Department Computer system which had malfunctioned for almost two years. A new system has been created that is
compatible with the rest of the Grand Lodge Office, and particularly the
Accounting Department. We are now in the process of reviewing and
checking the information in the database for accuracy and completeness.
This process has been a long and difficult one. but in the long run the new
system will prove its value to the lodges and to the Grand Lodge. In the
future we will be able to develop even more sophisticated means of communication with our lodges that have computer capability because our new
system is reliable and accurate.
I am also pleased that I had an opportunity to meet many of the leaders
of our lodges through the Wardens' Management Retreats. both last year
and this. In addition. our Assistant Grand Secretary traveled throughout the

144

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

to attend. The feedback we received through these contacts helped us
improve the operation of the Grand Lodge Office, and brought us closer to
our goal of Service to the Craft in California. Please accept my thanks for
helping to make your Grand Lodge Office successful this past Masonic Year.

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

APPENDIX E

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

175

REPO RT OF THE GRAND LECTUR ER
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California :
In a fraternity such as ours, the quest for, and eHorts toward, uniformity
in the ritualistic work of our constituent lodges is an ever present challenge.
Our Grand Lodge, since its inception, has long recognized the importance
of striving for uniformity in the work and conformity among the constituent
lodges. A system of instruction has been developed over the years whereby
our adopted ritual is taught by the Grand Lecturer and the Ritual committee,
through the lnspectoral corps, to the constituent lodges and brethren. Many
long hours and a great deal of eHort is expended each year by dedicated
Assistant Grand Lecturers and Inspectors and devoted Officers' Coach es.
My Brethren, I wish that I could report to you that our present system is
achieving the desired results in all cases
In our travels throughout our jurisdiction, we have found some deficien cy in the way that ritual is ponrayed. Our Grand Master in addressing the
State of the Craft, noted a continued deterioration in the quality of oHicers'
work and has charged the officers of the constituent lodges to recommit
themselves to quality ritual Your Grand lecturer and Ritual Committee join
with our Grand Master in commending those who present the work with
pride and spirit and in asking for a recommitment to quality from those who
need improvement.
At the same time, we recognize that many of the problems ot the
fraternity stem from the unwillingness or unavailability of brethren to serve
as oHicers. We wish to express our thanks to those who do step forward to
assume the burden of leadership, with a special thanks to those dedicated
Past Masters who often serve several times. Their continued dedication has
often meant the difference in their lodge's survival. And while we can otter
no solution to the problem of lack of officers. please be assured that your
Grand Lecturer and Ritual Comminee stand ready to assist in any way
possible.
While I am concerned about the quality of the ritual work within our
jurisdiction. I am heartened by the reception of our seminar program. The
ever increasing requests for these seminars and the growing attendance at
them are evidence of a desire to learn This program must be continued
and expanded .
The goal at each of the levels within our system of instruction continues
to be excellence and uniformity In the presentation of our ritual . Any success
that we may enjoy in advancing toward that goal is due 10 the many
dedicated brethren who give so unselfishly of their time.

176

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

I wish to thank those selfless brethren who serve as Otticer's and
Candidate's Coaches. This first line of instruction is truly a labor of love.
No expression of appreciation to those who serve as Inspectors can be
adequate. Each year has brought this dedicated band many added duties
and responsibilities and I continue to marvel at their amazing capacity to
serve. They teach. they counsel, they plan , they assist. Their dedication,
devotion, leadership , and love of the Fraternity are an inspiration to us all
The Ritual Commlnee joins me in thanking these brethren for a job well done.
No Grand Lecturer of this jurisdiction can hope for any success in the
fulfillment of his responsibilities without the strength and support of his
colleagues on the Ritual committee. I have been privileged to have served
this past year with six of the most ded icated members of our Fraternity and
I am deeply saddened at the loss to the Comminee of four of these Brethren
The recent passing of Brother Samuel E. Harris, Assistant Grand Lecturer,
Division V, after so many years of dedicated service is a loss not only to the
Ritual Committee but to the Craft, and l know that the Fraternity joins me in
offering our sincere condolences to his wife Charlsie. Sam's passing
together with the retirement from the Committee of Howard E. Moore,
Assistant Grand Lecturer, Division 11, Reuben F. Herrold, Assistant Grand
Lecturer, Division IV, and Edwin A Robinson, Assistant Grand Lecturer.
Division Vl. leaves a great void that will be difficult to fill . These brethren are
among the finest minds of the Craft and they will be sorely missed . Their
service over the years has made a great impact not only upon the Fraternity
but on the personal lives of those of us who have been privileged to have
worked most closely with them. I wish to add my sincere personal thanks
to those of a grateful fraternity not only to Sam. Howard. Reuben and Ed.
but also to their ladies Charlsie, Lois, Wilda, and Virginia who are and have
been beloved members of our Grand Lodge Family.
In closing, I wish to express my personal thanks to our Grand Master
for his demonstrated trust and support; to the Grand Lodge Ofticers who
have acquitted themselve s so ably. It has been a pleasure to have served
with you. To the Grand Secretary and his staff for their continued and
valuable assistance: to all who have assisted the Ritual Committee in their
appointed tasks this past year. there are many. And finally, to you my
brethren, for allowing me the privilege of service to the Fraternity.
Respectfully submitted,
Ulysses G. Patropulos. Grand Lecrurer
