179

GRAND ORATION
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

My brethren, every year at the Annual Communication of Grand Lodge
your Grand Orator has presented to the members of that august body some
topic appropriate to the moment. Indeed my first assignment to this office
last October was to attempt to memorize the minuscule portion of official
ritual which described the specific duties of the Grand Orator and so doing
directs him to "Deliver at each Annual Communication an address to the
Grand Lodge on matters appertaining to the Craft ..."
"Matters Appertaining to the Craft"
Alas, this phrase so briefly describing my assignment gives hint to my
quandary and substance to my indecision. Namely, what do I feel are topics
so vital for our reflection that I place them before you at this very special
time. I must confess my problem in preparation was not that so often faced
by anguished authors. namely, the stubborn silence. the inability to bring
forth some cogent thought, some single word. some black upon the glaring
white of empty page. No my brethren, quite the contrary. I was then as I
am now immersed in limitless thoughts that I wish so much to share with
you before this magic moment, this brief but oh so rewarding time for me is
done. And so my theme is not a single note but rather a melody that though
brief in passage pays homage to that symphony we call Freemasonry! If I
have worked my craft correctly perhaps some of you will leave this assembly
with bits and pieces of the turn upon your lips! In any case I pray not to
offend your senses with even a single discordant note!
Let me begin by clarifying for the record the use or misuse of the term
"oration" as it pertains to modern Masonry. In the vernacular of "today" it
has been the victim of "bad press." Among most of us it has taken on a
negative aspect like "advice from one's mother" or medicine that everyone
takes but doesn't really believe in. But in ancient times i1 was the practice
to honor a glorious event with a laudatory address given by someone who
would frame the remarks with 'high language.· We have permined this
practice to deteriorate into an imagery of pomposity at worst and boredom
at best.
Well hopefully this past year in the Grand Lodge we have attempted to
modify this image. Masonry is after all at least in its roots a very ancient
structure. Furthermore. regardless of the busyness we ascribe to the daily
problems of management, membership and lodge mechanics that occupies
most of our conscious energies ·there is something quite individual. quite

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

become occupied with the leadership of the Fraternity are understandably
guilty at times of forgetting the fact that our Masonry, regardless of its
outward emphasis upon fellowship and good works, deals primarily with the
individual candidate and calls upon the Great Architect to witness all of our
transactions. In short, at the heart of it, ours is a very sacred and personal
work.

I suggest that every Mason carries within his very being his own lodge
room, his own corp of officers, his own work, and that our duty, my brethren,
our assigned task given to us the moment we first knocked at the outer door
of our material •·mother lodge," is to remain at labor! If no other point be
made let it be this: i1 was ordained that the Temple be completed not by
angels and not by a stroke of Divine Magic but by the hands of man, and
that work has not yet been done! Until it is, we need never question the
purpose or timeliness of our Institution!

180

It is in this context that our Grand Master interpreted the role of his Orator
this year and permitted him at each ceremonial occasion to bring us back
to that thread of spiritual continuity that like an underground spring, silently
and without fanfare nourishes all that resides above it. I wish personally to
acknowledge this fact and to thank the Most Worshipful Stan Cazneaux for
not only permitting but directing us in these labors. In fine. Masonry
deserves an oration as a means of brief reflection. even if those of us who
are so assigned fall short in fulfilling our tasks.
But let us move on now to our present assignment, the message for
today. I do so by stating at the onset my conviction from which I derive no
small measure of energy, namely that :
Freemasonry represents a task which is to date not only unfulfilled yet
holds within its boundaries the destiny of the human race.
As such and in keeping with the spirit of our Grand Master's emphasis
this year on renewing interest and seeking excellence, we must conclude
that our role or duty is once again to become "operative Masons" in every
respect! Operative Masons again!
Therefore. there is for me no validity whatsoever in questioning
Masonry's relevance. Indeed, there is rather a concern along with a touch
of sorrow that too many of our members are still unaware of their heritage
and consequent duty. Like young Prince Hal early in his career in
Shakespeare's Henry IVth, most Masons do not know the role that is theirs
to play. The difference is that the young soon-to-be King of England was
unaware of the great domain over which he would reign while I suggest that
most Masons mistake the boundaries of the lodges to which they belong
and over which they preside! And I feel it is only proper at the convening of
the Grand Lodge of Masons of California. when the leadership of our august
body meets to deliberate and plan for a new Masonic Year, for a new cycle
of service. that we pause to refl ect again upon our sacred origins. And by
origins I do not mean our history in terms of days and years. but rather
principles, those fundamental dynamics that have always been the source
or basic breath of our lives.

181

It is time that we also lay to rest the misconception that the essentials
of our work are secret. Secret not only to those who are not members of
the Fraternity but to many Masons not privy to some obscure and revealing
text. The truth of the matter is that the tenets of Masonry along with their
published symbols being in their essence dynamic reflections or expressions of the wisdom, power and justice of our Great Architect are by their
very nature a secret only to those who will not ask; an enigma only to those
who do not think: and a limitless yet unavailable resource only to those who
will not serve! In the words of the great Mason and scholar Albert Pike: We
are here to think. if we can think and to learn, if we can learn!
If we in the Grand Lodge have discovered anything during the past
twelve months in meeting our brethren in both large and small communities
throughout thi~ jurisdiction as we joined to lay cornerstones. dedicate
edifices. rededicate lodges , burn mortgages, open clinics. recognize assorted acts of community and social services. and yes perform degrees in
the newest and most modern of lodge halls as well as open lodges surrounded only by mountains and trees. it is, my brethren. that Masonry is not
speculative, it is operative' Operative! We labor today to build; to build an
environment not only for our future generations but for today: a society
which today cares for its indigent, today insists upon justice and equal rights
for all of its citizens. today supports our public schools. and today pays
homage to those laws and principles which guarantee us the essential civic
freedoms'
To grasp the real essence of my message for us today we must look
beyond the external "operative" mode that we observe by merely following
the trestleboard of our respective lodges or as we interact with the community at large. Let me therefore guide us for a brief moment to the threshold
of that inner lodge, that lodge which dwells within the breast of not only each
of us as members of this honorable society. but all who in the Wisdom of
our Great Creator, walk in His image. For it is neither you nor I but only the
Grand Master of the Universe who determines membership in that lodge
room!

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

Let us not be misled by this lack of our exclusivity my brethren. There
is no loss in our personal prestige to recognize that in spirit · Masonry,' as a
moral science and an honorable Order dedicated to the Fatherhood ot God
and the Brotherhood of Man. has been practiced by albeit too few but
nonetheless select groups in every society that has graced civilization
independent of their physical membership into our Fraternity. Indeed all
Masonic historians will avouch that it is precisely this fact, that is to say the
universality and timeliness of our symbols and traditions. I stress again
symbols and traditions we have shared with others. which gives credence
to our claim that Masonry prevailed hundreds. indeed thousands of years
before the creation of any Grand Lodge.

were not and are not unique in setting apan a holy spot for the meeting . the
all-important meeting - between mortals and the Great Creative Source. If
that spot is not properly prepared, then the invisible spirit spirits which move
the streams, carve out the mountains. and hold steadfast the heavens will
not appear and grace the eyes of man with its or their presence. Furthermore. it is important for us to recognize that the liturgies of our most modern
and sophisticated religious systems practiced by us today also share this
same fundamental principle: the need for a sacred space, a "Holy of Holies,"
a Kadosh. Kadoshim." And I might add. it is this dynamic, this preparation
and calling forth for a Divine Presence that innervates and gives meaning to
our Masonic practices and ceremonies associated with Dedications.

Remember this fact in all our dissertations: we daily justify our doctrines
by proving beyond any question or doubt whatsoever. that our Masonic
traditions rely upon. indeed are limited or edited expressions of all the great
philosophic and religious systems that have punctuated civilization since
the earliest age of humanity . This is one of the purposes of Masonic
research. to discover and document the depth. vitality and richness of our
family tree. This is an imperative point worthy of reiteration if for no other
reason than that it answers in the aHirmative the question whether or not our
order is relevant to the present age. Let it be clearly understood that we are
an oral textbook of the Great Ideas· that have propell ed the human race
and this fact alone is sufficient answer to those recent few voices who with
bl inding passion accuse us of transmitting a questionable doctrine. Putt ing
it bluntly and succinctly, Masonry is made of the stuff we coll ectively call
civiization and culture. It is as much a part of that grand organism named
"humanity · as are atoms to molecules and molecules to matter'

From the psalms of David to the Teachings on the Mount: from the Tau
of Lao-Tse to the medita1ions of Gautama Buddha; we are asked to "prepare
ourselves· to receive a doctrine that is in essence the source of our being.
I mention this only to call your attention to our Masonic lodge room and the
preparation that it takes to bring our candidate to that special place, to that
singular moment and to that proscribed process by which he alone hears.
acknowledges, accepts. and in so doing "is made a Mason"

182

Let us continue our theme from a different perspective. Let us return to
the subject of our personal . individual lodge. One of the most fascinating
observations about primit ive societies. both those in the far past as well as
the present, is their need to create in their sacred life-cycle rituals what is
defined as a ··sacred space." By· life-cycle" rituals I mean the ceremonies
associated with birth, adulthood and death and including events such as
naming, circumcision, puberty rites. confirmation. marriage, and initiation
into select groups such as elders. chiets, or shamans, etc. Whether the
ceremonies be held within a lodge hall, a cave, atop a mesa. or within a
forest. the arena in which they recreate. as they all do, some legend of their
birth or epic of their God or Gods, must be clearly defined and set apart as
a special holy place. Tourists today travel to Greece to see the ruins of the
Elysynian Lodges. to India to visit the Elephanta caves. to the Outback in
Australia and the highland s of New Guinea to glimpse a hint of where the
Aborigines perform their rites. and trek the jungles of South and Central
America as well as Southeast Asia only to learn that our native Americans

183

Do you see my brethren how the Masonic process works? It does not
preach or dogmatize with man-made systems of thought that too often
divide us with the illusionary wall of 'separatism." Rather by a relatively
simple yet profound act at the altar. has us perform a ritual whic h tolfows
the life processes that humanity takes as it grows inward from ignorance
and exclusivism to understanding, comprehension. and unification. You
see brotherhood lives in the glory of knowing that in our hearts we are ONE!
The legends and symbols that we utilize are ancient and capable of
lengthy dissection. A lifetime can be filled analyzing and peeling away veil
after veil of meaning gleaned from meditating upon the square and compass, the Three Lights. the working tools. and the host of references included
within the Hiramic Legend. But so then is the Tibetan mandalla the subject
to millions of a life of meditation. Indeed according to Carl Jung. the eminent
psychologist. religious symbolism. tribal folklore, and even the hieroglyphics
of the alchemists are the stuff by which our unconscious attempts to speak
to and harmonize with our conscious bodies My point is that one need not
knowingly be a student of Masonic lore or esoteric studies to he a good and
true Mason! Nor are we as Masons the lone travellers journeying from East
to West in search of Light! We should be s1renyt11ened in our resolve by this
very recognition that we are part of a grand and glorious "Brotherhood of
the One Idea."

Masonry you see is not confined to the mind or to the intellect: it is more
imponantly a citizen of the heart and soul1
Within each of us is a holy altar at which we bow every time we care.

There is a Master and two Wardens and the attendant oHicers in full

be our intentions; no matter how passionate may be our cause. it is love and
not hate. it is sympathy and not impatience. it is understanding and not
disdain, and it is the embrace of affection and not the resistance of suspicion
that are human reactions in harmony with the very laws that not only keep
the galaxies in their orbits but also, I suspect. bring a smile to the Countenance of the Divine Author of the Universe!

Delivered at Grace Cathedral, October 11, 1992
By The Very Reverend Larry W. Coyle. Grand Chaplain
THE GREATEST OF THESE!
Scripture Text: I Corinthians 13: 1-7; 13.
No doubt. you have heard the expression. "The Greeks had a word for
it!"
For our English word, "LOVE'. they had four words that give all of the
shadings of our one word, distinctive definition.
In the English language, we shade the expression ot "LOVE' by the
context in which we use it. For example. we may say. "I just love ice cream."
or "We just love our new automobile." or "I really love my children" or "I really
love my wife or husband." or ·we love our friends." or "I love God." Placed
like that we know and understand that we love ice cream differently than we
love an automobile and we love an automobile differently than we love our
children. We love our children differently than we love our wife or husband
and we love our spouse differently than we love our friends and we definitely
love God differently than we love things. However, to some people. because
you use the same word. you may not love God any differently than you love
ice cream.
In the Greek language. with their four words that we translate "LOVE",
there was no such problem with shading of meanings. Their first word was
EROS from which we derive "erotica" or "erotic", describing the lowest form
of degrading, pornographic. fleshly or sensuous "LOVE". And for your
information, that particular word is never used in The Holy Bible. The second
word is, "STORGE" and it is only used once in the New Testament, in the
letter of the Apostle Paul to the Romans and there it is translated, "Be KINDLY
AFFECTIONED TO ONE ANOTHER, BRETHREN" denoting a warmth of
brotherty love. The most often used word is the word. "PHILOS" or
"PHILEO ·, when combined with the Greek word. "ADELPHOS" which means
"brother" becomes PHILADELPHIA (City of Brotherly Love) and is defined
by the love of husband to wife, parents to children and rriend to friend. mostly
describing all of the higher human aspects of love and affection. Then there
is the fourth word. "AGAPE", pronounced "AH-GAH'·PAY", which describes
the love of God ... LOVING AS GOD LOVES ... LOVING EVEN THE UNLOVABLE ... loving people in spite of themselves .. .loving people that under any
other circumstances you might not even like.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

For example, in John 3:16. which says, "For God so loved the world ... ",
the Greek word is 'AGAPE'. In I Peter it is recorded, "God is love". The
Greek word is "AGAPE". Ard in our Scripture text where it is recorded, "... the
greatest of these is LOVE", the word is "AGAPE". It means loving all, even
the unlovable by any standard that you or I can set. It is that which most
likely prompted a thiny year veteran missionary to the Sudan to ultimately
paraphrase and capture the essence of this greatest essay on love ever
wrinen. A well worn carbon copy was found and is shared here.

The Inspired Apostle Paul begins by declaring that a man may possess
any spiritual gift, but if that gift is unaccompanied by love... it is a useless
gift .. .and in God's sight is considered as nothing

190

'If I have the language ever so perfectly and speak like a pundit, and
have not the love that grips the heart, I am nothing. If I have
decorations and d iplomas and am proficient in up-to-date methods
and ~ve not the touch of understanding love, I am nothing .
If I am able to worst my opponents in argument so as to make fools
of them, and have not the wooing note, I am nothing. If I have all
faith and great ideals and magnificent plans and wonderful visions,
and have not the love that sweats and bleeds and weeps and prays
and pleads. I am nothing.
If I surrender all prospects. and leaving home and friends and
comforts, give myself to the showy sacrifice of a missionary career,
and turn sour and selfish amid the daily annoyances and personal
slights of a missionary life. and though I give my body to be
consumed in the heat and sweat and mildew of India . and have not
the love that yields its rights. its coveted leisure. its pet plans. I am
nothing, NOTHING' Virtue has ceased to go out of me
If I can heal all manner of sickness and disease. but wound hearts
and hurt feelings for w ant of love that is kind, I am nothing. If I write
books and publish articles that set the world agape and fail to
transcribe the word of the cross in the language of love, I am
nothing. Worse. I may be competent, busy. fussy. punctilious. and
well -equipped, but like the church at Laodicea---nauseating to
God."

One such gift is the g ift of tongues. that is languages that one has never

and Paul remarks that you may speak in a thousand d itterent languages by

NOTHING ... NOTHING! That is a stanling word when applied to a person.
Nothing .. .means not anything at all ... the opposite of something ...IT MEANS

Who is a nothing person? In God's eyes, it is that person who has no

That best portion of a good man's lite
His linle. nameless, unremembered acts

SIMPLICITY
Brethren, I have found three things that are impossible. One is to climb
a fence leaning toward you. One is to kiss a girl leaning away from you and
the third is to hold the attention of a fine audience like this after hearing from
the great speakers we have heard here today. However. I do have a
confession to make. In all of my life. I have never ever tried to climb a fence
leaning toward me. However... ... on several occasions ...I have tried to hold
the attention of a fine audience like this following outstanding speakers
Today, we are concerned with communication. It is amazing to see how
language has changed over the past few years. Further. it has come to my
attention that some of you have not been as faithful in your worship
at1endance as you might have been and I want you to know that you have
missed a great deal in your educational process by your absence. For
example. the arrangement of words in the church bulletins can be really
humorous. Here are some examples: All are authentic, taken directly from
announcements made in various churches:

* This afternoon there will be a meeting in the South and North ends
of the church. Children will be baptized at both ends.
• Wednesday. the Ladies Literary Society will meet. Mrs. Johnson
will sing. "Put Me in My Little Bed." accompanied by the pastor.
* Thursday. at 5 p.m .. there will be a meeting of The Little Mothers

Club. All wishing to become Little Mothers will please meet the
pastor in his study.
* This being Easter Sunday, we will ask Mrs. Brown to come
forward and lay an egg on the altar.
* The service today will close with 'Little Drops of Water'. One of
the men will start quietly and the rest of the congregation will join
in.
• The ladies of the church have cast-off clothing of every kind. and
they can be seen in the church basement on Friday afternoon.

See, how much fun you've missed by not being regularly in worship.
Further, the changes in language are even more radical than most of us
can suppose. An anonymous poet wrote:
"Remember when hippie meant big in the hips,
And a trip involved travel in cars. planes and ships?
When pot was a vessel for cooking things in,
And hooked was what Grandmother's throw rug had been?
When fix was a verb that meant mend or repair
And to "be in", simply meant being there?
When neat meant well organized. tidy and clean.
And grass was a ground cover, normally green?
When lights and not people were turned on and off

And the pill might have been what you took for a c~ugh?
When groovy meant furrowed with channels and hollows
And birds were winged creatures like robins and swallow~?
When fuzz was a substance. fluffy like lint.
And bread came from bakeries and not from the mint?
When a roll was a bun and a rock was a stone,
And a hang-up was something you did with the phone?
When chicken meant poultry and bag meant a sack.
And junk was trash, cast-offs and old brick-a-brack?
When cat was a feline ... a kitten grown up,
And tea was a liquid you drank from a cup?
When a swinger was someone who swung in a swing.
And a pad was a sort of a cushiony thing?
When way out meant something was far. far away,

And a man couldn't sue you for calling him gay?
Words once so sensible, sober and serious,

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

196

Now. make the freaked out psyche. delirious
It's groovy .. man ..groovy, but English it's not.
Me thinks that the language has sure gone to pot. ·
Simplicity. Keeping language simple and easily understood was one of
the great characteristics of President Abraham Lincoln. On April 7, 1865. at
11 .00 AM . he sent the following dispatch to General Grant.
Lieut. Gen. Grant,
Gen. Sheridan says, ·If the thing is pressed. I think that Lee will
surrender."
Let the thing be pressed ."
A. Lincoln
Grant, after reading, smiled in agreement. He did as he was ordered.
Exactly two days later at Appomattox Court House. General Robert E. Lee
surrendered . ''The thing was pressed" and the war was ended.
Simplicity. Profound , exacting, rare simplicity. Charles Swindoll wrote
that Lincoln was a master of it. His words live on because of it. When
assaulted by merciless critics. many expected a lengthy, complex defense
of his actions . It never occurred. When questioned about his feelings, he
answered. "I'm used to it. " When asked if the end of the war or some
governmental rehabilitation program might be the answer to America 's
needs. he admined quite simply, "Human nature will not change." In
response to a letter demanding the dismissal of the Postmaster General. he
wrote. ''Truth is generally the best vindication against slander.' When
encouraged to alter his convictions and push through a piece of defeated
legislation by giving it another title, he reacted with typical simplicity, "If you
call a tail a leg. how many legs has a dog? Five? No, calling a tail a leg, don't
make it a leg!'
I have heard that some ministers have the reputation of being long
winded but I never thought that that applied to me. until one Sunday when
I was half way through my sermon. a man got up and walked out. lt was a
little distracting but I continued. I noticed that the man stopped and spoke
to one of our ushers so I made a mental note to speak to the usher later.
After the service I went to the usher and said, "l saw you speaking to the
man who left the service early. Why did he have to leave?'', to which the
usher replied, "He said he needed a haircut." I quickly responded, 'Well,
why didn't he get one before the service?' And the usher stated. "He said
he didn't need one."

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

197

Simplicity. The difference between something being elegant or
elaborate. The difference between class and common. Between just
enough ard too much. Between concentrated and d~uted Between communication and confusion.
Here's one example. Which do you think is better
"Hence from my sight...nor let me thus pollute mine eyes with
looking on a wretch like thee. thou cause of my ill; I sicken at thy
loathsome presence ... "
Or simply.
"SCRAM I"
Simplicity. Economy of words mixed with quality of thought held
together by subtlety of expression. As Charles Jehlinger. a former director
of the American Academy of Dramatic Arts. used to instruct all beginning
actors with five words of advice:
"Mean more than you say."
The lo~ger Lstudy our Master Teacher's method of communicating. the
more convinced I become that His genius rested in His ability to simplify and
clarify issues that others confused and complicated. He used words anyone
could understand ...not just the initiated He sa id just enough to inspire and
motivate others lo think on their own, to be inquisitive, to search further.
And he punctuated his teaching with familiar, earthy, even humorous illustratio.ns that riveted mental handles to abstract truths. Best of all. .. He didn't try
to impress people. All these things led others to seek His counsel and thrive
on His instruction.
If He could summarize my thoughts for me today, I believe He would
offer this advice:

* Make it clear.
* Make it simple.
* Emphasize the essentials.
" Forget about impressing .

* Leave some things unsaid.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

We've got one of the greatest messages on earth to declare. Most
people have either never heard it or they've been confused because someone has complicated the issues. As I believe, our Master Teacher would say
and perhaps said through Abraham Lincoln. "If the thing is simplified, they
will surrender."

ANNUAL COMMUNICATION

Delivered by ~rry W. Coyle, Grand Chaplain
COOL SKEPTICISM!
This day, brethren. I feel like a mosquito in a nudist colony. I know what
to do. I just don't know where to start.
There is so much skepticism in our world . People have a hard time
believing what they see and hear. I am reminded of the burglar who was
robbing a home and while he was on his knees pilfering through some
valuables. he heard the panting breath of a dog behind him and turned to
see a huge Doberman. with fangs bared. looking directly at him.
Perched atop the Doberman's head was a little green parrot and the
parrot said. "Hello." and the burglar said, · Hello." and the parrot said . "Hello."
and the burglar said ''Hello." and the parrot said, "Hello." and the burglar
said, "for goodness sake. can't you say anything except 'Hello"? To which
the parrot replied. "Sic 'Em·.
Nine-year-olGI Danny came bursting out of Sunday school like a wild
stallion. His eyes were darting in every directi0n as he tried to locate either
mom or dad. Finally. a~er a quick search. he grabbed his daddy by the leg
and yelled, "Man. that story of Moses and all those people crossing the Red
Sea was great!" His father looked down. smiled . and asked the boy to tell
him about it.
"Well, the Israelites got out of Egypt, but Pharaoh and his army chased
after them. So the Jews ran as fast as they could until they got to the Red
Sea. The Egyptian Army was genin' closer and closer. So Moses got on
his walkie-talkie and told the Israeli Air Force to bomb the Egyptians. While
that was happening. the Israeli Navy built a pontoon bridge so that the
people could cross over. They made It!"
By now, old dad was shocked . "Is that the way they taught you the
story?··
"Well, no, not exactly," Danny admrned. "but if I told it to you the way
they told it to us. you'd never believe it, Dad.''

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

With childlike innocence the little guy put his finger on the pulse of our
sophisticated adult wor1d where cool skepticism reigns supreme. It's becoming increasingly more popular to operate in the black-and-white world
of facts ... and, of course, to leave no space for the miraculous.

. . Then what about miracles? Well, let's limit them 10 a child's world of
f1ct1on and fables. And if necessary. lo stained glass sanctuaries where
emotions run high and imagination is needed to make all those stories
int er~sting. After all, what's a little religion without a pocketful of miracles.
And .1f we started trying to account for all those things in the Bible, think of
the t1m~ it would tak~ t~ explain stuff like how the sun stood still, or why all
those fish filled the d1sc1ples' nets or the children of Israel crossing the Red
Sea on dry land?

200

It's really not a new mentality. The Apostle Peter mentions it in one of
his letters in the New Testament:

".... .I want to remind you that in the last days there will come scoffers
who wHI. .... laugh at the truth. This will be their line of argument : "So the Lord
promised to come back, did He? Then where is He? He'll never come 1
Why. as far back as anyone can remember everything has remained exactly
as it was since the first day of creation." (2 Peter 3.3-4, TLB)
Skeptics think Iike that. If they could choose their favorite hymn, it would
certainly Include the words, "As it was in the beginning, it is now and ever
shall be .... "
TAKE GRAVITY. Heavy objects tall toward earth. Always . So a builder
can construct a house and never worry about his materials Heating away.
Count on it.
TAKE CHEMISTRY. Mixing certain elements in precise proportions
yields the same results. Always.
TAKE ASTRONOMY. The sun. the moon. those stars work in perfect
harmony. Always. Even the mysterious eclipse comes as no surprise.
TAKE ANATOMY. Whether it's the pupil of the eye expanding and
contracting in response to light or our skin regulating our body temperature
or our built-in defense mechanism fighting disease. we operate strictly on
the basis of facts. Hard. immutable, stubborn facts. Reliable as the sunset.
Real as a toothache. Clear as an X-ray Absolute, unbending, undeniable.
People who conduct their lives according to such thinking are called
smart. They haven't a fraction of tolerance for the supernatural. To them it
is sloppy to think in terms of the unexplainable, the "miraculous." If insurance companies choose to leave room for ·acts of God,'' that's their
wagon and they have to pull it... but those are fightin' word s in laboratories
and operating rooms and scientific rap sessions and among newspaper
editors.

201

What would have happened had Moses tried to figure out all that was
needed to accomplish God's command?
.

One of the biggest mathematical miracles in the world would be required
They had to be fed, and
feeding three or three and a half milfion people would require a lot of food.
in th~ desert, what was he going to do with them?

According to the Quartermaster General in the Army, Moses would have
n e~ed 1,500 tons of food each day. That much food would fill two freight
trains ... each a mile long.
Besides, you must remember. they were out in the desen. and they
would have needed firewood to use in cooking the food. This would take
4,000 tons of wood and a few more freight trains each a mile long, just for
one day. And they were forty years fn transit.
Oh yes, they would have needed water. If they only had enough to drink
and wash a few dishes, it would have taken 11 ,000,000 gallons each day ...
enough water to fill a freight train 1.800 miles long.
And then another thing! They had to get across the Red Sea in one
night. Now if they went on a narrow path, double file, the line would be 800
miles long and would require 35 days and nights to get through. So there
had to be a space in the Red Sea 3 miles wide. so they could walk 5,000
abreast to get over in one night.
Think about this. Every time they camped at the end of the day, a
campground two-thirds the size of the state of Rhode Island was required.
or a total of 750 square miles.
Do you think Moses figured all this out berore he left? I think not. You
se, Moses believed God. He had faith that God was going to take care of
these things for him. According to the Holy Word, He did

Another thought. what about Jonah and the whale? The story is told of
the minister who was walking down the street and ran into the local skeptic
who challenged him abou1 his belief. The skeptic said , "I just don't see how
you can believe all of those stories that are printed in that Bible." Th em inister
responded, "What do you have in mind?" "Well," he said. ''What about that
old story of Jonah and the whal e? Can you really believe that some man
was swallowed up by a giant fish. lived three days and then was thrown up
on the beach?" The minister replied, "I sure do. As a matter of fact, if I was
a betting man, I would just bet you five dollars that is exactly the way it
happened." The skeptic asked, "Well, how would you prove it?" "That's
easy," said the minister. ''when I get to Heaven. I'll just ask Jonah." "Sut,
what if he's not there? ', came back the skeptic. To which the minister
replied . "Then you ask him."
Smart. keen-thinking skeptics don't have to worry about explaining little
things like that. It's easier to simply embrace a wholesale denial of the
miraculous ...which is fine and dandy... until they themselves get sick. face
death, and need miraculous help crossing that final river.
What happens then? Hey, if I told you what the Sible really says. you'd
NEVER believe it.
Let us take courage; we have the same God. We think our problems
are so big . Remember what God can do! Just team up with God . Drop
your skepticism and put your problems. like Moses. in His care. Let Him
run your life. and as the old song says. "He will take care of you."

Grand Master Cazneaux's Decision
Removal of Master of Foothill Lodoe No. 564
Shortly after my installation as your Grand Master. I met with Robert B.
Eckstein, who was then serving as Master of Foothill Lodge No. 564 and
with Donald Schauer, Jr., the then Secretary of that Lodge The purpose of
that meeting was to discuss the contents, preparation of and mailing, prior
to the 1991 Annual Communication of this Grand Lodge, of a letter advocating the election of a specified Brother as Grand Secretary
Although the subject letter was not signed by Brothers Eckstein and
Schauer, but was instead signed by the wives of each of these brethren. it
was admitted to me by the said brethren that the letter was thus prepared
and signed as a result of discussions had by the brethren and their wives
after the Inspector of the District had specifically told the brethren that if they
were to author and mail such a lener, it would constitute a violation of Section
2074 of our California Masonic Code and subject them to charges of
Unmasonic Conduct. They admitted, further. that copies of the letter were
mailed to addresses obtained from the copyrighted Roster of this Grand
Lodge as furnished to the Lodge with postage provided through the use of
the non-profit mailing permit obtained for official Lodge use.
Based upon my findings arising, in the main, from the brethren's own
admissions to me and based, further. upon their total lack of remorse, I
decided that, in furtherance of my duty as Grand Master to exercise a careful
supervision over the Craft and to see that our law was strictly obeyed, that
good reasons existed for disciplinary action. Spec~ically. I found that each
of the said brethren had been negligent in the proper performance of his
respective duties in that:
1. By permining the non-profit mail ing permit, issued by the United
States Postal Service to the Lodge for Lodge business use. to be used for
the mailing of the subject 'campaign" letter constituted either a violation of
Federal Law. if the letter was to be considered as "non-Lodge business" or
a violation of CMC Section 2074, if it was considered to be proper Lodge
business.
2. Having been advised by the Inspector of the District that the distribu t ion of such a letter by a Mason would constitute a Masonic offense under
CMC Section 3308. the d iscussion of the matter by the brethren with their
wives and the ultimate preparation of the letter to be signed by the wives
.
. .

3. The failure on the part of the said Master and Secretary to protect
the confidentiality of the Roster of this Grand Lodge and to properly
safeguard the use of the Lodge's non-profit mailing permit was illustrative
of a neglect of duty.

In a spirit of Masonic benevolence, I issued an Edict directed to Brother
Eckstein sening forth a series of conditions which, if met, would save him
from being suspended as Master of his Lodge. Basically, a letter of apology
was to be prepared, signed, and submined to me for approval prior to
November 30, 1991 . Said letter was thereafter to be mailed . at no expense
to the Lodge. to each Lodge in this jurisdiction prior to December 7, 1991
and Brother Eckstein was to apologize to his Lodge at its December Stated
Meeting. Said Master was also instructed to remove Brother Schauer as
Lodge Secretary and to satisfy certain other conditions. Brother Eckstein
was specifically advised that a failure on his part to satisfy all of the conditions
and to report his compliance to me prior to December 10, 1991, would result
in his immediate suspension as Master of Foothill Lodge.
Brother Eckstein did remove Brother Schauer from his duties as
Secretary of Foothill Lodge. and did make an honest attempt to apologize
to his Lodge at the December Stated Meeting Unfortunately, Brother
Eckstein chose to defy his Grand Master and to ignore a major portion of
the Edict. No letter of apology was ever prepared nor submitted to me as
required.
Failure of Brother Eckstein to submit the required letter of apology to
me by the November 30, 1991 date made it impossible tor him to comply
with all of the conditions of the Edict. After numerous attempts by the District
Inspector to open up a line of communication between Brother Eckstei n and
the Grand Master. Brother Eckstein still refused to write or make a phone
call to me.
Regrettably because of his non-compliance with the terms of my Edict,
therefore, and for good reasons shown, I notified Brother Eckstein on
December 6, 1991, that he stood suspended as Master of Foothill Lodge
No. 564 ettective that date.

Report of rhe Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

The Committee on Policy and General Purposes supports the Grand
Master in his Decision No. 1.
We point out that the Grand Master was most generous in his decision
merely to suspend the Master of Foothill Lodge No 564. The circumstances
enumerated by the Grand Master, in the opinion of this committee, could
well be considered to be in violation of Masonic Law and the penalty
thereunder could have been much more severe.

Because of his non-compliance with the terms of the Edict. the Grand
Master notified Brother Eckstein, prior to the Lodge's installation, that he
stood suspended as Master of Foothill Lodge No. 564. ""*

We recommend approval of Decision No 1.

Pursuant to the provisions of Section 1016 of our California Masonic
Code, the Grand Master has the power to suspend the Master of any Lodge
from the exercise of the powers and duties of his office, for good cause. until
the next Annual Communication and thus the Grand Master acted within his
powers in suspending the Master of Foothill Lodge No. 564 Your Committee on Jurisprudence finds that this Decision is consistent with our law and
does not require an amendment to our Code.

Grand Master Cazneaux's Recommendation No. 1

As a result, a majority vote only is required for approval The question
of whether or not such suspension of the Master is to be made permanent
is a matter for the Grand Lodge to decide. An approval of the Decision will
have this effect.

Decision APPROVED.

Amendments to New California Masonic Code
At the 1991 Annual Communication. the Special Committee on Code
Revision presen~ed for adoption by this Grand Lodge. the revised California
Masonic Code which it had been prepari ng since its appointment following
the 1988 Annual Communication
Pursuant to the request of the Special Committee, this Grand Lodge
adopted the new Code but provided that it would become effective following
this. the 1992 Annual Communication.
The intervening year was to provide an opportunity for other committees
to review the new Code and to point out any apparent errors or omissions.
Such a review has been completed and a number of modifications have
been recommended to me to clarity language or to incorporate in the new
Code matters which appeared in the 1975 Code but were inadvertently
omitted from the text of the new Code.
I am satisfied that the text of these modifications do not constitute or
contain any substantive changes in our law but are in the nature of
"housekeeping matters" which will make a superb work product even better.

the California Masonic Code (1991 edition) as set forth in detail in the

Grand Secretary.

Since the last Annual Communication, the new Code has been reviewed
and we are in agreement with the changes which have been presented to
you. We feel it is important that these revisions be approved.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The Special Committee on Code Revision completed their work on the
revisions to the California Masonic Code prior to the 1991 Annual Communication. It was their intention to ask Grand Lodge to adopt the new Code
and authorize its distribution
However, since the changes to the Code were substantial in text and
format. and since our Committee had not had an opportunity to review the
work. your Committee on Policy and General Purposes asked that adoption
be delayed, or. if that was not acceptable, that the Code be adopted with
the provision that it would not become effective until the close of the 1992
session of Grand Lodge. The Code was adopted with this stipulation.

Discontinue Committees on Masonic Code and Review of Proceedings
as Standing Committees of Grand Lodge
As the membership of this Grand Lodge declines and our cost of
operating increases. it is incumbe~t upon us to activ~ly seek to increase
efficiency and to eliminate duplication so as to result 1n the best value for
the dollars expended.
In this r~gard. it is important that we use the experience gai~ed from
past years of operation to examine the committee structure of this ~rand
Lodge and eliminate those committees whose functions we can do without
or whose functions can be performed by another existing committee.

Since the Grand Secretary's office is, by the very nature of its operation,
constantly in touch with the other Grand Lodges of the world and is kept
informed of the events occurring in those Grand Jurisdictions. it is my
opinion that the existence of a separate committee to duplicate this work is
a luxury that we can no longer afford.

Grand Lodge.

1991 Annual Communication
Grand Master Sherod's Recommendation No. 1
Eliminating Proficiency Requirement - Third Degree
It is my personal observation that. however well intentioned the requirement in our current law may have been no known benefits are realized from
requiring a Master Mason to pass a satisfactory proficiency before applying
for membership in any concordant or appendant organization as has been
the law in respect to any member raised in our jurisdiction after October
1953. To the contrary. in fact. this requirement has often been a known
deterrent for men interested in joining our Fraternity when they learn that
such a proficiency requirement is required . Experience has demonstrated
that this added burden of a Third Degree proficiency examination has not
demonstrated the intended suppon for the members remaining active in
their Lodge. Furthermore. we must, realistically. observe that those Grand
Lodge jurisdictions that do not impose t his requirement have not suHernd
as a result of not having a restriction imposed upon their membership.
Accordingly, it is my recommendation that our California Masonic Code
be amended by reporting Section 2635thereof. We would, thus, be eliminating the existing requirement of a Third Degree proficiency as a precondition
to joining our concordant or appendant organizations.

Since this Recommendation at the last Annual Communication received
less t~an the five-sixt_hs vote required for adoption but did receive a majority
vote. rt has been carried over for reconsideration at this Annual Communication and a two-thirds affirmative vote is now required for adoption.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
. Not all candidates who receive the Third Degree return their proficiencies. Of those who do so. experience has shown that except for those
bre.thren wh~ go on ~o become officers of their Lodges, very few retain in
their memones that ritual which they have learned.
Under Section 23200 of the California Masonic Code, those who do not
return th~ir proficiency are denied the opponunity to join any of the concordant bodies, and are thus barree from furthering their Masonic education
and from enjoying the good fellowship of the members of those concordant
bodies. That ~egi~lation enacted in 1953, was adopted to endeavor to keep
members active 1n their constituent Lodges. rat her than lose them to the
concordant and appendant organizations The effect of this law has been
to hinder rather than promole goodwill and harmony among the various
Masonic organizations and Lodges.
Your Committee on Policy and General Purposes agrees with Grand
Master Sherod's Recommendation No. 1 and recommends a "YES" vote.

Report of the Committee on Jurisprudence:
Grand Master Sherod recommended that. because there appear to be
no known benefits being realized from requiring a Master Mason to pass a
satisfactory proficiency before applying for membership in a concordant or
appendant organization but that, contrariwise. said requirement has often
been a known deterrent for men interested in joining our Lodges when they
learn of such a profic iency requirement. the existing requirement of a Third
Degree proficiency as a precond ition to joining our concordant or appen-

. We have been asked by your Ritual Committee to report they concur
wrth the Comminee on Policy and General Purposes on this Recommendation .

Grand Master Sherod 's Recommenda tion No. 2

d. Lodge mailing lists or Grand Lodge rosters are not
furnished to advertisers:

Relief On Fraternal Advenising

e. no representation is made to Masons that is their duty
to advertise;

It is, in my opinion. time to revise our thinking regarding the use of
advertising in order to defray certain of our operating expenses. The cost
of operating a Lodge today is too expensive and "creative" ways must be
found to ottset some of those expenses.
Other jurisdictions have for years permitted the use of advertising as a
source of funds to assist in defraying expenses. This jurisdiction, in its earlier
years, did the same. While it is true that abuses were found to exist which
gave rise to our present prohi bition, we, perhaps, went too far in attempt to
solve our problem . I believe that bycarefuldrahi ng of our law we can control
the possibility of abuses and still have the much needed benefits of advertising income.
It is my opinion also that we should concentrate on putting our own
house in order and, in respect to the matter of advertising, we should let our
concordant bodies address their own needs.
It is my opinion also that we should concentrate on putting our own
house in order and, in respect to the matter of advertising, we should let our
concordant bodies address their own needs.
It is therefore my recommendation that the first paragraph of Section
2072 of our California Masonic Code be deleted and the following language
added in place thereof:
Sect ion 2072. A Masonic Lodge may permit advertising by its
members and businesses or commercial establishments owned or
operated by its members, in Lodge publications as a means of
offsetting the cost of publ ishing, printing and mailing such publications. provided that :
a. the advertisements are in good taste and whhin the
bounds of propriety;
b. advertisements for controlled substances. alcohol or
lobacco are nol accepted;
c. advertisements for religious or political groups are not
accepted;

223

f. solicitation of advertisements is by a member in good
standing of the Lodge for whose publication advertising is
solicited : and

g. any excess over the expenses of publishing, printing and
mailing such publication is donated to a recognized
Masonic cha rity.
Report of the Committee on Jurisprudence :
Grand Master Sherod recommended that. because the cost of operating a lodge today is becoming more expensive: because other jurisdictions
have for years permitted the use of advertising as a source of funds to assist
in defraying expenses, and because he believes that . by careful drafting. our
law can be changed so as to make available the benefits of advertising
income while still avoiding the abuses which occurred prior to our present
restrictions. our Cal ifornia Masonic Code be amended so as to permit limited
advertising by lodges and concordant bodies to defray the cost of their
publications.

Repon of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
For the benefit of those delegates attending a Grand Lodge Annual
Communication for the first time. a brief history of advertising in this
jurisdiction will assist you in deciding which way you should vote.
In the first half of this century, advertising in publications which were
purported to be Masonic in character had run rampant. However. it is
important to realize that those publications did not originate from the LocJges
and other Masonic organizations. They were commercial publications
containing hundreds of small advertisements by individuals and businesses.
The very existence of those publications depended on solicitations of
advertising from members and others. High pressure tactics were used.
and many other abuses were experienced

225

In the past 30 years. legislation has been proposed on several occasions
to make changes in the advertising restrictions. In 1976. some relaxation in
the prohibition was approved to allow the insertion of commercial advertising in charitable football programs (such as those sponsored by the Shrine) .
with the provision that these advertisements be limited to a statement of
congratulations or best wishes from the advertisers. without mentioning a
product.
For many years. the California Masonic Code has allowed youth groups
to advertise their events and to solicit advertising from the public but has
denied this privilege to its adult groups.
Grand Master Sherod's Recommendation No. 2 is the result of refine·
ments to a simtlar Recommendation in 1989 which was generally favored
by this committee except for the use of agents to represent the Masonic
organizations in the matter of advertising. The Recommendation before you
has eliminated the use of agents.
We believe that the concerns of the past have been properly addressed
in this Recommendation and that the safeguards provided will ensure that
the problems of the SO's will not re-appear. The adoption of this Recommendation will be an important step forward in assisting Masonic groups
with their financial problems. Your Committee on Policv and General
Purposes recommends a · Yes" vote.
Recommendation APPROVED .

Resolution No. 91-11
Permits The Wearing Of A Past Master's Apron While
Occupying An Otticer's Chair On Past Master's Night
This proponents of this Resolution seek to provide that when occupymg
an officer's chair on Past Master's Night, a Past Master may wear his Past
Master's apron in lieu of the apron of the station occupied.

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M of California:
WHEREAS. the Grand Lodge of California and its various committees
have performed outstanding work in recognizing the many members ot the
constituent Lodges for their individual contributions to Masonry in general

and to their respective Lodges in particular: and
WHEREAS, it is a known fact that a Master, on his retirement from that
time honored chair, is given such accolades as would befit him; and
WHEREAS. a Master's dedicated service to his Lodge is a mark of
distinction that continues throughout his life: and
WHEREAS, he is usually given a beautiful Past Master's apron to wear
on special occasions; and
WHER EAS , the proponent's of this Resolution do not believe that the
wearing of these aprons is in conflict with the existing Masonic code of dress:
and
WHEREAS. it is the internal and not the external prerequisites which
make a good Mason.
NOW, THEREFORE , BE IT RESOLVED that the Ritual of this Grand
lodge be amended to provide that when a Past Master occupies an officer's
station on the occasion of a Lodge's Paste Master's Night. he shall be
allowed to wear his Past Master's apron.
Respectfully submitted,
Emmett F. Hill, PM No. 297
Donald A. Christenson, PM No. 310
Chesler F. Conley, PM No. 297

Nathan W Spears, PM No. 297
Fernando P. Felix. PM No. 297

Permits Closing With All Members On Level

John A. Scheich, SW No. 378

To paraphrase Section 2690 of our Masonic Code and Section 26340
of the Code adopted in 1991 as amended at this Communication. the Grand
Lodge has the power to authorize cha nges in our ritual, but only for a single
purpose: that purpose being lo have the ritual remain as it has been handed
down since time immemorial.
There may come a time when delegates to this Grand Jurisdiction will
be of a mind to a;,,end our law so that, for whatever reasons. changes could
be made in its ritual. Your committee. however. urges that such a potential
amendment be viewed with extreme caution. because to allow changes in
the ritual, for reasons other than those allowed by our present law. could
well result in ritualistic chaos. For example, our law now provides for the
adoption of a specific cipher ritual. but only insofar as that cipher ritual
conforms to our present ritual. Periodic changes to the ritual could create
diversions in the adopted specific cipher ritual. Changes in ritual would
make i1 extremely diHicult for the members of the Ritual Committee and t he
lnspectoral corps to be certain of the prevailing ritual in order to be able to
instruct as to which portions of the cipher ritual conform and which do not
so that Lodge officers could be properly taught. Indeed, it would be dijficult.
if not impossible. to maintain a uniform ritual throughout the jurisdiction.
There has been no evidence presented to this Grand Lodge that our
ritual ever provided for having all of the otticers and members come to the
floor of a Lodge at its closing ceremonies. thus meeting on one level.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

Obviously. the proposed change cannot return the ritual to a form which
never existed. It follows. therefore, that the proposed change is contrary to
our Masonic Law and should not receive favorable consid eration by this
body.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this legislation seek to amend Section 25010 of the
1991 California Masonic Code to provide for the annual election of officers
during the November Stated Meeting.
While reasons are cited in the resolution to advance the election to
November. they would not mitigate the problems presented by the
proponents as the Senior Warden must plan for his year as Master long
before any November election, nor are the reasons sufficient to require every
lodge in the jurisdiction to change its election date.
The present legislation as provided in Section 25010 or the 1991 California Masonic Code provides that a lodge may. by a standing resolution.
adopted after notice in writing to every member of the lodge. approved by
the Grand Master, and effective six months after approval. provide that the
annual election may be held at the Stated Meeting in November each year.

As you make your decision on this Resolution. we ask that you try to
put aside the emotional reactions to this change, and let reason guide your
vote. Please consider one point which we haven't previously mentioned.
Some months ago, our Grand Orator was explaining. at a ceremonial
O_Pening of the Grand Lodge. our use of symbols to teach Masonic principles. He. of course. mentioned our physical symbols. the square. the
compass. etc .. but he went on to include our floorwork as symbols - as
"symbols in space." Think about that concept for a moment . "symbols in
space."
Since we teach by using symbols. ask yourself which is the most
symbolically meaningful position for the candidate to hear the lecture and
r~ceive his charge. We believe that it is in the symbolic center of the lodge,
with the Three Great Lights of Masonry prominently displayed before him .
We hope you agree. and we request your 'No · vote.

Resolution No. 91.~11
Permits Use Of Cipher Ritual At Tiled Meeting
Called For Practice Of Ritual
The proponent of this Resolution seeks to allow the use of a cipher ritual
at a tiled meeting called for the purpose of practicing the ritual except for
those participating in the ritual.

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& AM of California :
WHEREAS, the possession of a cipher ritual book is no longer illegal in
this Jurisdiction; and
WHEREAS. the Grand Lodge of California has published its authorized
version of the ritual through the approved cipher ritual book. and
WH EAEAS. it is extremely important for the members of this Jurisdiction
to find ways to exemplify the ritual in a more uniform way with inspiration:
and
WHEREAS, Resolution 90-28 did not go far enough in outlining who is
permitted and who is excluded from using the cipher ritual book at certain
practice meetings; and
WHEREAS, If officers are expected to perform better at a faster rate,
thereby enhancing the Candidate's understandi ng of our ceremonies, ritual
cipher books must be permitted at all practice sessions.

The legislation as distributed to the Constituent Lodges in 1991 is not
now adequate to carry out the intent of the proponents because of the
adoption of the new California Masonic Code which changes the numbering
and wording of our law but does not change the substance of that law. To
carry out the intent of the proponent and to conform to the numbering and
language of thE! new Code. your Committee on Jurisprudence submits the
following Resolution:
RESOLVED that the last sentence of Section 26300 of the California
Masonic Code (1991 edition) is amended to read as follows·
"lo no eyent shall any cipher ritual or portion thereof be used during
a tiled meeting except where such meeting is called for the purpose
of practicing the ritual. Such use shall be permined only by members who are not particioating in the oresentation of the ritual."
Since this Resolu1ion at the last Annual Communication received less
than the five-sixths vote required for adoption but did receive a majority vote,
it has been carried over for reconsideration at this Annual Communication
and a two-thirds affirmative vote is now required for adoption.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
At present, our Code allows the possession and use of a cipher ritual
and, in no event, shall any cipher ritual be used during a tiled meeting.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

244

The proponent seeks to limit the use of a cipher ritual in a tiled meeting
called for practice to those members not participating in the ritual
The Comminee on Policy and General Purposes is of the opinion that
this legislation is not needed.
In 1987, the Grand Lodge considered legislation to allow the possession
and use of a cipher ritual in California. The matter was carried over to 1988
and was then adopted. A related resolution introduced in 1988. prohibiting
the use of a cipher ritual during a tiled meeting, was also adopted at that
time.
In 1990, a Resolution was introduced which would have provided for an
exception to allow the use of the cipher ritual for the purpose of practicing
the ritual in a tiled meeting. That Resolution was carried over to 1991 but
was not adopted.
The present Resolution is basically the same but also restricts the use
of the cipher ritual to those members not participating in the ritual. The
proponent does not clarify who would be considered participants.

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

245

Aeporr of the Committee on Ritual:

Despite the carefully developed definition of what constitutes a Tiled
Meeting, it is our opinion that any meeting which is not open to non-Masons
is '1iled. · In that context. a meeting called for practicing the ritual is certainly
'tiled.' That is what this proponent had in mind when he wrote the Resolution
last year
Experience has demonstrated to the Comminee on Ritual that the
presence of cipher rituals at practices is not disruptive to the conduct of
those meetings. On the contrary. their controlled use enhances the education process.
We supported this Resolution last year. and we again unreservedly
encourage your "Yes" vote.
Resolution ADOPTED.

1992 RESOLUTIONS

You will note that this Resolution refers only to practice in a tiled meeting.
It does not address the situation in which practice sessions are held in places
other than a tiled lodge.

Authorizes Use Of Square And Compasses By Sciots

We define a tiled lodge as one at which a tiler is on duty, the lodge is
duly opened and minutes are kept.

The proponents of this Resolution seek to authorize the Sciots 10 utilize
the square and compasses on a lapel pin together with the Sciots emblem.

We believe that the use of the cipher ritual during practice sessions is
an appropriate means of achieving the high degree of proficiency expected
of the lodge officers Practice sessions may be held in any location and
would rarely, if ever. be held In a tiled lodge.

Resolution No. 92-01

WHEREAS. in 1905, a group of Master Masons met in San Francisco
for the purpose of forming a social club. free from the restraint of the Lodge
Room. yet organized for the purpose of furthering Masonic teachings and
applying, in a practical way, in everyday business affairs. the teachings of
the fraternity. This club was to be know as the Boosters; and

The committee is convinced that the legislation in Section 26300 of the
1991 California Masonic Code which prohibits the use of the cipher ritual in
a tiled meeting is adequate to achieve the intended purpose. namely to
prevent officers from reading the ritual from the cipher book during a regular
meeting.

WHEREAS. in 1910, the Boosters adopted a Ritual based on the ancient
cult of Stoics whose custom was to work in unity in all things and changed
the name to Sciots; and

Furthermore. it is our opinion that detailed rules regarding the use of the
cipher ritual in practice sessions are unnecessary and should not become
a part of the Masonic Code.

Reporr of the Commirtee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this resolution seek to permit the Ancient Egyptian
Order of Sciots to incorporate the square and compass into a lapel pin
together with their own emblem.
The Sciots is a Masonic Organization formed in the early 190o·s for the
purpose of promoting social and Masonic relationships. They stress the
importance of Masonic teachings. and encourage their members to incorporate those lessons in their dealings in everyday life. Since the Sciots are
a part of the California Masonic Family, your Committee feels that this
request to use lhe square and compass in the design of their lapel pin is in
harmony with the policy of this Grand Lodge
Your Committee on Policy and General Purposes therefore recommends a 'Yes" vote.

Reporr of the Committee on Commercialism:

WHEREAS. it was suggested by the Community Awareness Committee
of the California Masonic Family Forum that all members of the Forum use
the slogan. "A Part of the California Masonic Family."

The Comminee on Commercialism feels that the use of the square and
compass by another Masonic organization is improper. The square and
compass is worn proudly by all Master Masons. and can and should be worn
by all, even though an individual is eligible to wear another emblem.

NOW. THEREFORE, BE IT RESOLVED. that the Ancient Egyptian Order
of Sciots be authorized to use the Masonic Square and Compasses on a
combination lapel pin together with the Sciots emblem.

The Resolution does not appear to have any adverse effect on the
advertising. or commercialism restrictions of this Grand Lodge. and we give
no opinion as to a problem in that area.

Respectfully submitted,

We feel that this Resolution should not be passed. since we all proudly
wear the square and compass now. and therefore urge a "No'' vote.

WHEREAS. an amendment to the Lodge By-Laws must be presented

for discussion and voting at the very next Stated Meeting ; and

Report of the Committee on Jurisprudence:
The proponents of Resolution No. 92-05 seek to require written notice
to be given to all members of a Lodge when an amendment to the By-Laws
has been proposed and is to be acted upon.

GRANO LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The California Masonic Code Section 19400, Article XI II presently
provides that an amendment to the by-laws may be made at any Stated
Meeting by a two-thirds vole of the members present. if the amendment has
been presented, in writing, at the preceding Stated Meeting. There is no
requirement that all members be advised of the proposal.
Your Committee on Policy and General Purposes firmly believes that
good communication is essential in any organization and that the member·
ship-at-large has a right to know what changes are being proposed in its
by-laws.
For reasons which are not well known, the anendance at most Lodge
Stated Meetings averages only about 10 percent of the membership. If the
only communication of a proposed amendment to the by-laws is the reading
of such written proposal at a Stated Meeting "to those present," it is obvious
that the other 90 percent of the members will be unaware of the proposal.
Involvement of members in the affairs of the Lodge may generate
interest in the Lodge activities, increase attendance and result in a more
active, successful Lodge. This Resolution is an important step in that
direction.
As the proponents point out . Resolutions to amend the Masonic Code
of California are sent to all Lodges well in advance of the Annual Communication so that the delegates have time to form their opinions on pending
legislation through discussion in the Lodges
It is therefore reasonable that such a general procedure should be
applicable to the Lodge's consideration of amendments to its by-laws.
The Committee on Policy and General Purposes endorses this Resolu·
tion as a positive contribution to good business practice as well as to the
improvement of fraternal relalions.
A "Yes" vote is recommended.
Resolution ADOPTED.

This Resolution seeks to amend our law and a five-sixths affirmative vote
is required for adoption.

Report of the Committee on Jurisprudence:

The proponents of Resolution No. 92-06 seek to authorize a Lodge to
contribute Lodge funds to assist a Lodge or Temple Association suffering
losses from catastrophic events such as earthquakes or fires.
The legislation as distributed to the constituent Lodges is adequate to
carry out the intent of the proponents.
This Resolution seeks to amend our law and a five-sixths affirmative vote
is required for adoption.
Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

In 1983. Grand Master Harry Maynard reported that during the year,
three of our Lodges had suffered catastrophic losses; Kauai Lodge No. 589.
from Hurricane Eva. Coastside Lodge No. 762 from a devastating flood and
Coalinga Lodge No. 387 from a major earthquake. He noted that he had
sent nominal funds from the Grand Master's Relief Fund to assist in two of
these disasters. but the real need was greater than our Fraternity could
provide.

257

At the next Annual Communication the special committee recommended that a disaster relief fund be created to provide emergent and
short-term relief to constituent Lodges which suffer property losses from
disasters. The fund would be created by an assessment of five cents per
capita per year until the sum of $50,000 was accumulated. The per capita
would then be discontinued until such time as the fund needed to be built
back up to the $50,000.
One argument against this proposal was that the primary responsibility
of protecting Lodge property should be with the Lodge itself through
adequate insurance coverage. There was also a reluctance to add to the
on-going increases in the per capita tax Accordingly, the proposal to
establish a disaster relief fund was defeated.
We now have a proposal which would permit Lodges in this jurisdiction
to make a contribution from Lodge funds to any constituent Lodge or Temple
Association which has suffered damage or loss as a result of a natural
catastrophe. This would be accomplished by adding this as a discretionary
expenditure to Section 21080 of the California Masonic Code.
We find this proposal to be consistent with our policy of self-sufficiency
and with the practice of Masons voluntarily helping one another.
Your Committee on Policy and General Purposes recommends a "Yes"
vote.
Repon of the Committee on Finance:

Resolution No. 92-06 proposes to authorize contributions from a Lodge
to assist another Lodge or Temple Association which has suffered a
catastrophic loss. Your Committee is of the opinion that financial matters
of the constituent Lodges are not appropriate for consideration by the Grand
Lodge Finance Committee. Its concern is only with Grand Lodge finances.
However. since this Resolution was referred to your Finance Committee.
we would point out that the proposed addition to Section 21080 of the 1991
California Masonic Code does provide a control by use of the term
"reasonable sum." In addition. this Code Section already provides for similar
discretionary expenditures by the Lodges.

Report of the Commirtee on Jurisprudence:

Repon of the Committee on Finance :

The proponents of Resolution No. 92-08 seek to have the age require·
ments for applications for the degrees reduced from 21 to 18 years of age
for the sons and step-sons of recognized Masons and for members of
DeMolay.

Resolution No. 92-08 proposes to have the age of sons. stepsons and
DeMolay members. for the Masonic degrees reduced from 21 years to 18
years.

The legislation as distributed to the constituent Lodges is adequate to
carry out the intent of the proponents.
This Resolution seeks to amend our law and a five-sixths affirmative vote
is required for adoption.

The financial impact, if this Resolution should be adopted, is dependent
upon the number of applications received. We do not have any statistics as
to the number of males in this age group who would apply for the degrees.
Since the adoption of this Resolution would have no effect on the
finances of Grand Lodge as none of the fees inure to Grand Lodge, this
Comminee again makes no recommendation.

Repor1 of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

This is the seventh time in the past twenty years that an effort has been
made to reduce the minimum age of applicants from 21 to 18. The proposals
have taken a variety of forms. Some recommended reduction in the age
requirement for DeMolays. Others for sons of Masons and still others. for
all applicants. Grand Lodge rejected all of these proposals for two basic
reasons.
Some maturity is required rt a candidate is to understand the lessons of
our symbolic degrees. While maturity varies from one individual to another,
this Grand Lodge has consistently supponed the requirement of twenty one
years. There is no evidence, from the experience of other grand jurisdictions, that a reduction in age would increase our membership. There is
some concern that an 18 year old might find Masonry disappointing,
resulting in a permanent loss to our Craft.
Another more compelling concern is the effect of this proposal on the
Order of DeMolay. The vast majority of the leaders of this organization are
young men between 18 and 21 . Reducing the age limit for membership in
our Lodges could have a major negative impact on DeMolay. Again. there
is no evidence that our Lodges will benefit from this change but there is
ample evidence that DeMolay will benefit from the continued participation
of these young men in this fine Order.
Your Committee on Policy and General Purposes recommends a "NO"
vote.

Report of rhe Committee on Policy and General Purposes:

As this legislation is wrinen, it does not permit, it requires a Lodge to
waive the initiation fee for DeMolays. Presumably the Lodge would be
responsible for the fees which are required to be transmined to Grand

This is not a new issue to come before Grand Lodge. A similar resolution
was introduced in 1990. Your Comminee on Policy and General Purposes
opposed this prior resolution.
In the rare instances in which a DeMolay expressed a desire to join a
Masonic Lodge. but was pressed financially. there are nearly always Brother
Masons who are more than ready to provide the funds. thus obviating the
need for preferential treatment of DeMolays by legislation such as this. We
question the wisdom of rushing a 21 year old into applying for Masonic
membership, even if he has had experience in DeMolay.
Also. our initiation fees have lagged so seriously behind inflation that it
is very difficult to make a case that they are so high as to be burdensome.
Forty years ago, the fees were. in most case. the equivalent of about a weeks
wages or even more. Today, even those working for minimum wage, it
represents only about three days work . In most other cases considerably
less.
To try and induce DeMolays to join our fraternity by offering ··bargain
basement" fees seems condescending. That which is cheaply obtained is
cheaply valued.
We recommend a "No' vote.

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
In 1983 the Grand Master recommended that a Table Lodge ceremony
be prepared for Lodges to use as they desired. That recommendation
generally received the support of your Committee on Policy and General
Purposes, but failed to receive sufficient affirmative votes of the delegates
to gain approval of the proposal as presented

267

Also. this proposed addition to the ritual would require even further effort
and memorization by Lodge officers. especially those who desire to present
a Table Lodge at a Table Lodge ceremony at a First Degree.
This resolution, as presented. does not conform to current California
Masonic Law. This proposition constitutes a change to the Ritual of this
Grand Lodge which is prohibited under Section 2690 of the California
Masonic Code and Section 2631 O of the new Code (as amended) which
becomes effective at the close of this Communication These sections bar
changes to the ritual with one sole exception. that being to have the ritual
remain as it has been since time immemorial . Since California has never
had a Table Lodge ceremony, it is impossible to return such a ritual to a form
which obviously has not been handed down from "time immemorial "
Your committee is of the opinion that a Table Lodge Ceremony could
be of benefit to the Fraternity and a resolution to adopt a ·ceremony· as
opposed to changing the ritual could receive favorable comment at that
time, provided there was no requirement to open lodge on any degree as
part of the ceremony.
A 'No ' vote on the current resolution is recommended
Report of the Comminee on Ritual:
Before discussing this Resolution, we need to make a small. but important, distinction Strictly speaking, a Table Lodge is a formal. tiled lodge
meeting, often involving degree ritual, held around a dinner table. In fact, in
the early years of Masonry, the First Degree was conferred in such a lodge.
As our ritual work evolved, though, it became apparent that our purposes
would be better served by conducting our degree ritual in the lodge room
and leaving the eating and drinking to another time and place.
This developed into the ceremony with which we are familiar in California. the one involving a meal and a series of ceremonial toasts. Although
we commonly refer to it as a Table Lodge. it properly called a Festive Board.
We believe that this Resolution addresses this Festive Board. With that
distinction In mind, in order to conform to the wording of the Resolution. we
will continue to refer to it as a Table Lodge.

268

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

This Resolution would require two things.
(1) To formally open a Table Lodge on the First Degree. and
(2) To prepare a specttic rltual tor use at a Table Lodge.
Table Lodge meetings are currently being held throughoul the jurisdiction so they obviously are permitted by our law. We have personal
knowledge of at least half a dozen. Although no two use exactly the same
ritual. each form has, at some time, been approved by the Ritual Commit1ee
A Table Lodge is a social event, not a lodge meeting, and should be
allowed to follow whatever form is most appropriate to its purpose. We see
no reason to impose a set ritual on a Table Lodge. any more than we would
a Hiram Award ceremony. To insure that propriety is observed. we will
continue to require that the "ritual" part of every Table Lodge program be
approved by a member of the Commit1ee on Ritual
Your Commit1ee on Ritual feels that both a formal opening of lodge. and
a prescribed ritual. would be counter-productive to the purpose of a Table
Lodge and recommends a "No" vote.

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA
