Although the populalion of California has nearly doubled since 1960,
California Freemasonry has LOST nearly 100,000 members. and continues
to lose members at the rate of over 6.000 members per year We realize.
and acknowledge, that we do not have the answer that will put our fraternity
back into a "growth' situation rather than the ·decline" that has been going
on for several decades, but if we continue to think and act as if there is
nothing wrong, then this fraternity will cease to exist in California. Unlike the
Redwood Forest. and the gnat catcher in Southern California, the Federal
Government will not put Freemasonry on the endangered species list! We
have to act for ourselves. Let us. at least. make an effort to save our beloved
fraternity. This resolution may not be the answer. but if we do not try it, we
will never know if. indeed. this was what it took to start to put growth in
California Freemasonry.

There is no doubt that there is considerable sentiment for such modification. When Grand Master Leo Mark made Recommendation No. 7 in t 988.
which was basically the same as this resolution. it gained a majority vote.
and was carried over. but was not adopted in 1989.

NOW. THEREFORE. BE IT RESOLVED, that new proficiency examinations regarding the degrees be prepared by the Committee on Ritual. which
will require that the candidate repeat the obligation and demonstrate the
modes of recognition of the highest degree which has been conferred upon
him and that the obligation be repeated while kneeli ng in due form at the
altar.

271

The supporting argument for such changes usually comes from the
results of questionnaires sent to Entered Apprentices who did not advance.
When asked why, 75% reported, "too much time and effort to memorize the
proficiencies." Disregarding the usual tendency of responders to such
questionnaires to provide an answer that places a minimal responsibility or
blame on themselves. we would like to note t hat while time is certainly
limited, ettQI1 is closely linked to desire and commitment. We feel that it
would be equally easy to come up with the same excuse for not learning the
obligation and modes of recognition.
Before we are willing to see such an emasculation of our proficiency
requirements we would like to see some statistics from those several
ju risdictions that have so modified their proficiency requirements. Have
they experienced an upsurge in membership? Have they been successful
in having all appl icants progress through the degrees? Have those who
have taken the degrees under such circumstances become active Masons?

Report of the Committee on Policy and General Purposes:
The proponents of this resolution point out. quite correctly. that Masonry
has. and is. suffering serious membership losses. The gist of their argument
seems to be that we don't know whether or not this proposed modification
of our proficiencies will help. but let's try something.

We are facin.Q an either/or issue here. one with no middle ground.
Perhaps there are some questions in our proficiencies which could be
determined to be redundant or unimportant. However. to say that~ of
the material covered in the questions and answers is won hwhile having a
candidate learn is hard for us to accept. We are not ready to take the step
of excising from our required proficiency all that this resolution proposes.
Although one might take the position that this does not actually change
the ritual ; it simply excuses the ca nd idate from learning it, we feel that this
does indeed constitute a major. perhaps even a radical change in ritual. and
this resolution ignores the provisions of Section 2690 (new Code 26310) .
We must recommend a 'No · vote.

We feel that this imposes unnecessary and cumbersome floorwork on
what is intended to be a simplified proficiency, and must recommend a "No"
vote.

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M of California:
Most Worshipful Grand Master, Honored Guests and Brethren, on
behalf of the California Masonic Foundation. it is indeed a pleasure to report
to you again on the achievements made by the Foundation during the past
year.
I reported, last year, that several categories of scholarship funds have
been established to acknowledge special considerations of major donors.
These conditions may be geographical (such as students whose residences
are in a specific locality) , unique qualifications (such as for students who
excel in sports, or youth group activities, etc.). or students studying specific
subjects. These funds are typically designated, according to the donor's
wishes. by a specified name with all monies separately recorded and
accounted . Awards are made recognizing the specific fund and in accordance with the specific established conditions.
This program has proven to be very successful and has attracted
significant contributions to our scholarship programs. The current recession has had an impact on the earnings of the scholarship funds but the
major contributors have tended to offset the decline and the Foundation has
been able to maintain the current level of support to scholarship recipients.
All Masons should be grateful to these contributors who have allowed this
most rewarding program to continue to be successful. The Trustees would
truly like to see the stipends increased but this can only be accomplished
with an increased level of contribution by the brethren.
At present. in addition to the General Fund. the separate Funds administered by the Foundation include the following: The Amaranth Fund,
the Brotherhood Lodge Fund, the Grand Master's Fund (stated by our Grand
Master Cazneaux). the Chowchilla Lodge Fund, the Harry Walker Lister
Fund. the McElwain Fund. the Robinson Fund. the Soquel-Pajaro Lodge
Fund. and the Starr King Lodge Fund. The Foundation also awards scholarships with the income derived from the MacKechnie Fund for Grand Lodge.
To provide public recognition for major donations, this year we have
had prepared certificates of recognition. These certificates when framed in
8 ronze denote a single contribution of over $1,000. when framed in Silver
denote a single contribution of over SS,000 and over $10,000 when framed

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& AM. of California:

Your Board of Trustees meets quarterly and we have accomplished the
following this year:
SPECIAL STUDY· CALIFORNIA MASONIC MEMORIAL TEMPLE
For the past several years your Board has been concerned about the
need to increase the occup~ncy and profitability structure of your Memorial
Tempie and its entities in order to increase the financial base of our charitable
corporation. Your Board, therefore, at our July 1991 quanerly meeting.
authorized the Accounting Firm of Deloine and T ouche to conduct an
in-depth study of the operations and management of the Memorial Temple.
Garage and Pine Street Propenies for a fee not to exceed $35.000.
At the same time this study was being initiated we learned the Executive
Committee was likewise in the process of considering a similar study.
Consequently thereafter members of the Executive Committee attended our
Quanerly Board Meeting during the course of this study wherein we received
progress reports.' Former tenants. the San Francisco Convention Bureau.
booking agencies, and local hotels were contacted by questionnaire or by
personal interview to determine why such former tenants had not returned
and utilized the Memorial Temple facilities. From this report we learned the
strengths and weaknesses of our Memorial Temple operations of the proper·
ties.
This special survey study continued until January 29. 1992. when the
final repon was presented which indicated the following:
1. The auditorium was under utilized due to a very restrictive rental
policy. thereby not maximizing its profitability
2. An aggressive marketing and sales program under a professional
director could produce additional revenue.
3. The garage lease does not expire until March 31, 1993. and
recommends that the Board consider combining the operation of

the south and the Homes Administrators, representatives and medical statf.

Committee (four from the north and four from the south) have received the
applications for prospective residents and/or non-resident assistance.
These individual Trustees evaluate the applicant or recommend outreach

Policy

by Warren T. Buschmann, Chairman
During the year a partially updated Masonic Homes Policy Manual was
distributed to all Trustees. This was the first step of a process to review.
revise. and reformat the policy manual to ensure that each policy is current
and clear in meaning. In addition, the new manual contains a new Index
and Table of Contents to facilitate quick and accurate reference to all
policies.
A large number of the policies have been completely revised and the
Policy Committee expects to complete the entire process during the coming
year at which time the entire manual will be reprinted ancJ distributed.
Grateful appreciation to the other committee members, Verl Lobb and
Eugene R. Perkins, is acknowledged as is the assistance of all th~ Trust~es,
Paul Boyar and John Rose, Administrators. and all others for their contributions to the updating process.

Operating Budget

by Warren J. Blomseth, Chairman

The Financial Planning and Budget Committee of the Masonic Homes
Board of Trustees presently includes the Chairman and four members:
Warren T. Buschmann. Verl B. Lobb. William F. Stovall. and Ernest A.
Pooleon. The Chairman acknowledges with appreciation the continued
supporl. guidance and counsel provided by Roy J. Henville. President of the
Homes Board.
Last year's budget and financial report reflected a broad range of
improved service level s and related costs for both Homes. Thus. over the
past twelve {12) months this Committee and the operating and financial
staffs of the Homes have concentrated upon implementation with maximum
etticiency. This Committee. the Administrators. and Senior Staff are pleased
to report that at the close of the fiscal year June 30, 1992, the internal audit
of accounts reflected actual expenditures nearly 1% under budget. This was
accomplished in spite of a substantial over-run at the corporate level (San
Francisco Office) due to a new accounting methoo for Non-Resident Assistance. adjusted allocations, Foreign Tax on porlfolio transactions. and
unaccrued portfolio management fees. For next year we were able to effect
substantial cost savings and thus reduce costs year-to-year for the San
Francisco Ottice (see below)
Our two Administrators did an outstanding job of fiscal management
and were able to effect across-the-board cost saving efficiencies with
resultant savings as follows·

consider the ever-increasing cost of health care. Our Administrators came

Real Estate
by Eugene R. Perkins, Chairman

The Real Estate Committee of the Masonic Homes of California consists
of Eugene Perkins. Gerald A. Sharer and Verl Lobb.
As you may know. the Real Esta1e Committee is charged with the
responsibility of disposing of all real estate made available to the Masonic
Homes. through wills and bequests. gifts and grants.
During the past year, July 1, 1991 through June 30, 1992, the total
volume of sales amounted to $979,970.85. In addition. the committee also
reviewed oil and gas leases, notes receivable. and completed a number of
appraisals.

Contract signed by each new resident of the Home. This affords them a 90
day period to adjust and satisfy their own minds that they have no intention
of withdrawing from the Masonic Home. The resident then signs a release

petent C N.A.
As pan of our continuing plan of providing quality benefits to our
employees. we have authorized our Administ rator to explore the possibility
of offering a Flexible Benefit Compensation Program. If determin ed practical. employees choose the benefits they need and want. without wasting
compensation dollars on benefits that may be right for someone else.
1n addition, the Committee reviewed the employee pay ranges and
recommend a merit pool for this year's salary program. Adm inistrators were

by William W. Dickson, Chairman

UNION CITY
This year saw the completion of the dietary kitchen remodel. A difficult
job to complete - remodel the kitchen while keeping it in operation . There
were some surprises but the end product, as a result of the input and

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

patience of Brother Dennis Korpi, Dietary Director. and staH as well as the

Report of the Administrator

We had three areas of environmental concern due to underground
tanks. One area was the Sedam Building emergency oil tank which has
been cleared by regulatory authorities because it was found that the initial
test reading was incorrect A secord area involved an abandoned concrete
bunker which supplied oil to the old south boilerhouse. It has been cleaned
up at considerable expense. The third area is a gasoline tank by the north
loading dock which was abandoned and filled some years ago under the
supervision of the Union City Fire Department. We currently have several
ground water monitoring wells in this area and it remains an open problem.

1991-1992

292

We had a second analysis made of our existing North, South and
Administrative buildings for their condition and ability to resist earthquake
damage. This analysis was based on developments by John Kariotis,
Structural Engineer. using an Italian method, furthered by a grant from the
National Science Foundation. This approach tries to balance ground motion, structural response to displacement forces, life safety concerns and
defined levels of economic damage. We are situated only a few hund red
feet from a major fault, the Hayward. but we are resting on a large rock mass.
This can be expected to absorb a considerable amount of any energy
generated as opposed to alluvial type soils which caused significant damage
in the Loma Prieta earthquake.
We will be doing some precautionary strengthening in the coming year.
COVINA
The adut population is slowly growing at this location. Growth has to
be done in increments due to staffing levels. etc.
Of major interest to all brethren is a Board decision to further develop
this site with a Skilled Nursing Unit and Assisted Living rooms.
While development of this scope will "fill" this site. it will bring to reality
the wish of some - a "Union City South."
My thanks to the other comminee members. Gerald Sharer. Warren
Buschmann, and Gene Perkins. whose efforts and advice amount to more
than a "couple of days" a month, and to Roy Henville. who worked right along
and helped to keep our efforts on track.

293

Masonic Homes, Covina

As your Administrator I look forward to writing this annual report. It
affords me the opportunity to reflect back and view the big picture - The
Masonic Homes of California. This is my twelfth annual report and my
backward view provides an inspired look toward the future.
Children's Home
California, unlike the majority of Masonic Homes in other grand jurisdictions. has maintained its group home for children. The implementation of
foster home care, the availability of Aid To Families With Dependent Children
funding, financial considerations and regulatory control by government has
influenced many jurisdictions to allow government supported agencies to
provide out-of-home placement for qual ified children in need
We have not only maintained our service but we have evolved into a
healing center for children who. for the most part. have experienced significant and painful domestic stress. We continue to advocate for children
and pay close anention to their health. education. welfare with a non-institutional life style in what is technically an institutional sening.
The availability of public resources and foster homes in local communities of California, together with the lack of accurate information about
the Children·s Home by Masons and their families. is the chief reason why
our population is significantly less than the 80 Masonically related children
we are licensed to serve.
Yet, despite the available public resources and foster care systems,
there are many children in need of Homes like ours. particularly siblings who
need to remain together within the same household. These are children
who. without advocates and artention to their health. safety, education and
welfare. will become at least burdens on society and at most violent
criminals.
Through the estate of a wonderfully generous Brother. Herman Tate of
Seafaring Lodge No. 708, funding has been received. the earnings from
which will ultimately allow the Masonic Homes of California to serve ch~dren
who are not Masonically related. These children would be sponsored by
constituent Masonic Lodges in this jurisdiction The Tate Fund alone wUI

294

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

not all ow us to serve children to the fullest extent possible. Yet we have faith
in the generosity of Masons and their families who will continue this endeavor. Brother Tate's gift as applied by Seafaring Lodge reminds me of a
saying by Samuel Johnson, "He who waits to do a great deal of g ood at
once, will never do anything." Charity is a duty incu mbent upon all men but
particularly upon Masons. This belief does not restrict charity to any person.
It is this reason why Masons and their families have historically seen the
need as well as the cause.

possible, the need for skilled nursing care. Government subsidiz~d and
wholly private agencies are influenced to increase health care services to
those outside skilled nursing facilities.

It is always a pleasure to associate with the Alumni Association. This year
some 200 "children" returned home for a wonderful reunion. During this
reunion, and after 59 years, those responsible for carrying off the chocolate
eclairs during the 1933 earthquake confessed. Ninety-eigh t alumni
authored a book on behalf of the Association, "A Mosaic Of Memories",
which isa wonderful recollection of memories of alumni living in the Masonic
Home during the years 1915 through 1945. We are proud to boast of many
successful alumni. Since 1899 we have raised 13 79 children which includes
those now in residency.
We hope to see this Home once again filled with happy and contented
children who appreciate the present opportunities and future chances the
Masonic Homes of California can provide.
Adult Home
On January 8, 1990, the adult program at Covina commenced its
operations. As projected, our population has grown to over 40 ambulatory
residents. The elderly in need and approved for admission will continue to
be admitted to the Adult Home at Covina and the population will ultimately
stabilize at between 75 to 85 re sidents.
The residential care industry is also evolving. Two departments in
California government regulate ad ult care provided by the Masonic Homes.
The California Department of Health regulates skilled nursing facilities. The
California Department of Social Services regulates Residential Care
Facilities (care of the "ambulatory") .
The trauma experienced by the elderly who transfer from home to
congregate care and then possibly to a nursing home can be grievous. The
high costs of hospitalization and skilled nursing continue to grow. These
factors have caused government to consider methods to keep the elderly
in their own homes or in congregate living systems and forestall, as long as

Masonic Home, Union City
1991-1992
As a health care professional it is gratifying to be associated with an
organization that makes such a significant d iHerence in the lives of so many
of our Brothers, their wives and widows who turn to us for relief. care and
assistance.
These are difficult times, economically and politically. It is precisely in
times like these that our Home has excelled and demonstrated our leadership and resourcefulness.

RESIDENTS RELOCATING TO UNION CITY

Our facilities are aging in place along with our residents. Significant

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

303

Thus, one of my goals this past year was to begin to unravel the
misinformation surrounding the Home and to help clarify any
misunderstandings that may exist relative 10 its operation and policies. To
this end. we updated the Question and Answer Booklet. It now answers 38
questions about the Home. its operations and when and how to apply. This
booklet is made available for all those who visit the Home or call for
information. I continued to spread the word by addressing and handing this
booklet out at three District meetings. two High Twelve clubs. various
Widows' Nights and the Junior Warden's Northern Retreat. Last fall. I wrote
an article for the California Freemason which generated over 45 calls to my
office. The word is getting out.
Another well kept secret is that the Masonic Homes of California
provides two other services to the Fraternity. Not all those who are distressed and in need can and should be helped at the ' Homes." Two services
not provided at Union City. but available through the Masonic Homes, are
Non-Resident Assistance (NRA) and advice and referral through the Depart ·
ment of Outreach Services (DOS). Many times. a better placement decision
is leaving the applicant in the community they are living in and providing
in·home services and/or supplemental monetary assistance tor rent and
living expenses. This is NRA. Many times monetary assistance is not the
need . Understanding the system and what's out there and how to access
it, is the issue. T~t is where DOS fits in.

2. ~cw Re~ident Wcknmc U:na.
3. Ouc~lion and An:.wa lfonUct <Jbout the Home, its opcr<1 lion and
admis!-ion p\1licics.
4. Ruk!. and Rcgulatinn~ for rc~itlenL-. living al the Homt: .

Employee moral was enriched with a Grand Employee Recognition
Program. Special gifts were awarded based on length of service. We are
graced with an excellent caring, loving group of employees, some of which
have been with us for over thirty years.
It became very apparent to me that many of the Brethren had a lack of
knowledge and understanding relative to the Masonic Homes. their policies
and purposes. It was common to hear from the Brothers I met, ··1 have been
a Mason for over forty years. I've never been to the Home. how much will it
cost?" "What is the down payment and monthly fees?" ''I heard you have a
two year waiting list.' ' Is it true that you have to be physically able to carry
your bags up the front ste ps to be admitted?· My conclusion from these
comments is that the Masonic Home is a well kept secret. especially from
California Maso ns.

In the three years that I have been your Administrator, there were rare
times that we have had to deny a Brother. his wife or a widow admission to
the Home. There is nothing more heart wrenching than to hear it said, "That
when I needed my Brothers they were not there." Understand that any
applicant not admitted to the Home is automatically referred to NRA and
DOS, who assist them from that point on.
In this respect, the Home needs the assistance and understanding of
all our Brothers. We can reduce these negative reactions if all of us who are
involved speak. understand and convey the same messages. Communication is the answer that is needed. We must inform all of the Brethren of the
true facts about the Home and all of its programs.
We want you to understand that the Masonic Homes of California is here
for all our Brothers and their widows. We will aid and assist the distressed
who are presented before us. But sometimes the Home is not the only
answer.

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

The times they are a changing. The definition of need is chang ing . The
Homes are responding to thi s change. With the graying of America. more
people are living longer As we age we spend more and more dollars for
health care. Before the advent of Med icare and Medicaid in the 1960' s. those
residents who were in social, medical, and financial need who applied to the
Home were healthier and younger. On the whole. their health care needs
were not the predominant need. Today people have been retired 20 years
and are now applying for admission. Their major need now is assisted living.
Our nation, the industry, and the Masonic Homes of California are not going
to be able to answer all our new challenges with 'business as usual. "

dressing. Residential Care are residents who are independent and only
receive supervision in their medical maners by going to our Doctor who
manages their total care.

304

The average annual cost for each patient in our Skilled Nursing Facility
is %50.000 per year. or 7.5 million annually for all 150 patients. It has been
estimated that nursing care twenty years from now may cost $300.000 a
year. per patient. or 45 million a year for our current census. If we proje1.:t
our future income versus our future costs, we can see a time. possibly, where
these lines will cross and expenses will exceed income. This will definitely
be true if we allow our Skilled Nursing population to grow beyond our current
capacity. For this reasons, we. as a rule. do not admit anyone directly into
the Skilled Nursing Facility The only exception is when we admit a couple
and one needs SNF care Another reason is that we are a life care
community that guarantees all services to our current residents. We must
have space always available in the Skilled unit for transfers from within our
facility .
We need your assistance and that of your Lodge and your Secretaries
to get the word out that potential applicants should not wait until the last
minute physically to apply 10 the Home. Also that the Home may not be the
only answer But if they acknowledge that their future plans include the
Masonic Home. they should not wait until they need Nursing Home care to
apply to the Home.
Our Homes provide lite care. Lrte care means that all care. except
hospital, (if hospital care is needed. it is supplied by hospitals located close
to the Home) will be provided to the resident at either Home. When someone
is admitted to the Home. all medical care is provided by a Physician selected
by the Home. There are three levels of care provided at the Home: Skilled
Nursing (SNF). Personal Care (PC}, and Residential. Skilled Nursing is care
that is provided by licensed personnel 24 hours a day. All activities of daily
living, from bathing, dressing, toileting and medications, are supplied .
Personal Care is provided by certificated Nursing Assistants Medications
are centrally stored and some minor assistance is provided with bathing and

305

You should be proud that the Home provides and supplies the finest
medical. residential and personal care services available in the industry
today.
As Masons. we have the moral imperative to find the best way to answer
the call. For the Home to serve all those who are in need. now. and in the
future, we must find the best tools and techniques. programs and systems
that will promote quality in a cost effective way. We must be able to aid and
assist when called. We can only do this ii you have the knowledge. The
expression "knowledge is power," is at1ributed to lord Bacon; but it had its
origin long before his time, in the saying of Solomon that "A wise man is
strong: yea, a man of knowledge increaseth strength."

Report of the

Income From the Permanent Endowment Fund

Masonic Homes Endowment Board

321

Income from the Masonic Homes Endowment Fund amounted to
$6.950.598.23 which was transferred to the operating accounts of the

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:

Prologue
Your Masonic Homes Endowment Board is pleased to submit its 61 st
Annual Report covering the fiscal year ending June 30. 1992 Through the
commitment of many of the Brethren of this great Fraternity to the support
of the residents of our two Homes. their contributions through their Lodges
to the Endowment Fund, were over one and a third million dollars. Also. the
amount of bequests. gi~s and memorials was the second largest ever
received in recent years ··over three and a third mill ion dollars. It was indeed
a very good year!

Reflections
The Masonic Homes Endowment Fund fiscal year of 1991·1992 was one
of ups and downs. Considering the depressed state of the economy, the
demands placed on our money available for contribution to other charities
and programs within Grand Lodge, and our continued decline in membership we are pleased with what we have achieved The contributions through
Lodges only decreased 1.5 percent while the membership decreases 4.0
percent.
The programs that were instituted four years ago to increase the
awareness of the Masonic Homes in the members of our Fraternity is
continuing to show positive results. Billfold sized calendars were sent to
each Lodge to be given to their members as a reminder to contribute to the
Endowment Fund . The brethren liked the calendar but they told us that they
were not very easy to read. The 1993 calendars have corrected that
problem.
The presentation of the book "The Greatest of These is Charity" - a
history of the Masonic Homes of California · is being widely accepted as one
of great enlightenment to both the new Entered Apprentice as well as the
seasoned Master Mason. This program was so successful that a second
printing of the book has been authorized by the Masonic Homes Board of
Trustees. Lodges can receive these books by contacting their Endowment
Board member who will be pleased to make the presentation to the new
Entered Apprentice.

324

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFOR NIA

The response to the Masonic Homes Endowment Lapel Pins and the
Appreciation Certificates is continuing to be strong. The Lapel Pin for 1993
is similar to the 1992 pin and should continue to appeal to our contributors.
It is in the shape of the map of California and a star locates our two Homes.
There are many pluses in the contributions of 1991-1992. In deference
to the state of the economy, we are pleased to report that the average
contribution per contributor increased to $21 .77, the average contribution
per member increased to St 0.05, and the percentage of Lodges making the
Honor Roll increased to 46.9%. However. the percent of members contribut ing declined to 46.2%.

Also of interest is the fact that of the six divisions in the jurisdiction. two
of them had increases in contributions. Division I and Division V had
increases of 5.0% and 5.8% respectively. Interestingly enough again these
two areas are economically different and at the opposite ends of the state.

Another point of interest is that almost half of the Lodges (238 out of
490) increased their contributions over the prior year. There were 49 Lodges
that had contributions under $100 less than their contributions of the
previous year. However. the Lodges with increases were not of sufficient
magnitude to offset the decrease in contributions from the other Lodges.

Planning For The Future

328

For Years Ending June 30th

The Individual Contribution Incentive Program is progressing very well
and the majority of the Lodges are participating in the program. We have
redesigned the billfold sized plastic calendar for 1993 to make it more
readable. On the side opposite to the calendar is the Masonic emblem and
inscription "lt's Up To Me In '93 - I'll Give To The Masonic Homes Endowment
Fund."

l

20 .

A number of calendars, in excess of the membership of each Lodge.
were again sent to the Secretary of each Lodge with the hope that they will
be included in the dues envelope (or Lodge Bulletin} to each Mason in
California. Hopefully, we will convince the non-participating 54% of our
members to contribute something to the support of the Endowment Fund.

(/)

a:

We still have discrepancie s in the reporting and recording of contributions and the number of contributors for Honor Roll recognition. Here is a
suggestion to the Lodge Secretaries - How about making your checks
payable to the ·California Masonic Homes Endowment Fund" and in the
"memo" section on the lower left side of the check indicate the number of
contributors . Think about it, that would be a permanent record!!!
In planning ttle printing budget each year we look at the historical orders
of pamphlets from the Lodges. One way that Lodges can help the Endowment Board is to order only the quantity of dues envelopes and pamphlets
that it intends to use in one year. Also. if your order is not rnled in a
reasonable time. please do not place another order until you have verified
with the supplier the status of your order.
The presentation of the book "The Greatest Of These ls Charity" to the
newly initiated Entered Apprentice has been a great success. There are
many Lodges in the jurisdiction that still do not avail themselves of this
program. Our Board members will endeavor to see that each newly initiated
Entered Apprentice has the opportunity to receive a copy of the history of
the Masonic Homes of California.
There is still the major issue that needs serious attention and help from
the Brethren. "How can we reach the Brethren who do not now contribute
to the Endowment Fund and convince them to help in the support of our
Home?" We often hear that many of our Brethren can't afford to contribute
because they are retired and on fixed income. We are sure that some of our
Brethren are in that condition but not 54% of our members! Even small
contribution s are solicited. Just think, if every member contributed the price
of a cup of coffee each month , every Lodge would achieve Honor Roll! If

330

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

any one has any idea of how to solve this problem. please contact any
Endowment Board Member and make your ideas known.
Honor Roll Tabs and Certificates
For those Lodges which have attained Honor Roll status of an average
of $9. 79 or more per member for the first time since 1988. will receive a new
framed certificate. All other Lodges that have made Honor Roll status will
receive a 1992 seal for placement on their certificates. The frames and seals
will be presented to the Lodge during the Annual Communication by a
member of the Endowment Board.

Epilogue
Our sincere and grateful thanks to Grand Master Stanley M. Cazneaux
and to the Grand Lodge OHicers, Board Members. and Committeemen for
their support of the Endowment Program. We also wish to thank the
Masters. Wardens, Secretaries and especially the Lodge Endowment Fund
Chairmen for their efforts in furthering this worthy program.

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFOR NIA

333

We also extend a very special thanks to the 61.812 Brothers who through
their continued generosity, contributed over one and third million dollars to
the Endowment Fund during this fiscal year.
We appreciate the splendid assistance and support we hav e received
from the Masonic Homes Board of Trustees. and the Grand Secretary's
office. And - to all of the staff of the Grand Lodge Office a hearty thanks for
all your help.
Once again I wish to offer my personal thanks to the Homes Endowment
Board Members for their dedication and devotion in the support extended
to me again during this past year as your Chairman. With all the new
demands presented to them, they have continued to respond with vigor and
enthusiasm and their efforts are most gratifying. It truly is the great people
of the Endowment Board who work so diligently that ensures the success
of our program.
My special thanks go to Jim Kemper. He has served the Board for nine
years with distinction and is now leaving . We all wish Jim future success in
all his endeavors and that he remains in good health .
Respectfully submitted ,
John M. Palmer, Chairman

APPENDIX J

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

345

REPORT OF THE COMMITIEE
ON COMMERCIALISM

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, f.& A.M. of California:
The committee has had little activity during the year. but has answered
some questions regarding rentals. and other Masonic related fund raising
proposals.
The committee has been available to assist the lodges and other Adult
Masonic organizations in their endeavors during the year
As has been the case the past few years. we have not received any of
the required Financial Reports of the Shrine supported football games as
required by California Masonic Code Section 2070-d. and can give no
opinion as to their adherence to our guidelines. We hope that these reports
can be forthcoming, to insure that the requirements of our Code are met.
Also. there have been no known public fund raising activities reported
to us under California Masonic Code Section 2073. and cannot report as to
their compliance with the Code
Respectfully submitted,
Robert C. Coe. Chairman
Douglas S Lawrence
John T. Ward

John L. Darms. Jr.
Neal E. Macurda

REPORT OF THE COMMITIEE
ON CREDENTIALS

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
Your Committee on Credentials is pleased to submit this report of the
143rd Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge.
Again we wish to express our thanks to the Grand Secretary and his
office for their help.

Respectfully submitted,

The cost of the Annual Communication held in October of 1991 was
approximately $276.000 . The budget for the Annual Communication to be
held in October of 1993 is $235.000. The Deputy Grand Master has planned
next years itinerary so that he can travel more on weekends and less during
the week. Lodges have been encouraged to plan events on a district-wide
basis rather than on a local Lodge basis. These plans will reduce the
incoming Grand Masters reimbursed travel expenses. During the next
Grand Lodge year. a California representative will be reimbursed for visiting
the Annual Communications of neighboring states only.
The budget presented for your consideration was mailed to all Lodges
in California on August 31st and is as follows:

John W. Dennis. Chairman
George E. Fuson. Jr.
Richard C. Ryan

The proposed budget for the next Grand Lodge year continues the
current level of expenditures. with three major differences· (1) the budget
for the 1993 Annual Communication will be reduced by $41 ,000 from the
1991 level. (2) the Grand Lodge Officers' travel expenses for next year will
be reduced by $29.000. and (3} the Grand Master's pins for next year will
be sold for $1 each rather than given away. for a savings of $20.000. These
cuts will save approximately $90.000.

Albert F. Koula
Byron C. Smith

REPORT OF THE
COMMITIEE ON FINANCE
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& AM. of California:
Your Committee on Finance has reviewed the audited financial statements presented by the Grand Treasurer for the fiscal year ended June 30,
1992 as required by the California Masonic Code.
Necessary supplemental appropriations for those items where budgets
were exceeded totaled $184.461 and were approved by the Grand Master.
The Grand Lodge Finance and Execut ive Committees recommend a
budget which covers two periods: July 1, 1992 to October 31, 1992 and
November 1, 1992 to October 31, 1993. Grand Lodge is changing its fiscal

IN SPECTORS' ORIENTATION

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& AM. of California:
This special commiMe was formed in January of 1991 at the direction
of the Grand Master as a first step toward the development of a full
curriculum for Inspector development.
Studies from other Grand Lodge committees have stated that the Grand
Lodge should provide greater emphasis. guidance and suppon to establish.
develop, improve and maintain the highest possible standards for. administrative. government and management of constituent lodges. Actions
taken that staned to fulfill this objective would be directed through the
Masonic Education Group. Since Inspectors have an historically close
relationship to the lodges and must suppon the above long-term polic_y or
requirement, this committee called the Inspectors Orientation Comminee
was created as a vehicle to inform and train Inspectors in a manner
consistent with the information and training period to the constituent lodges.
In his February 5, 1991 letter creating direction for this committee and
informing all the Assistant Grand Lecturers and Inspectors. Grand Master
Sherod reconfirmed the Inspector to be both the supevisor of ritual for the
lodges in his district and the direct representative of the Grand Master for
the management and supervision of those lodges in all other matters. _In
order to strengthen and improve the ability of the Inspector to ex~rc1se
supervision. this committee was created and charged. In 1991, a serie~ of
workshops were conducted to structure the program. In 1992. the _resulting
workshops were structured based on Inspector input and Executive Com·
mittee planning.
On Saturday. February 8. 1992, the Executive Committee adopted. in
principle but not detail, the repor1 results coming from the Inspectors input
through the 1991 semir1ars The committee activities of t 992 reflect that
general direction.

REPORT OF THE COMMITTEE ON

The Investment Co mminee has the responsibility of reporting annually
to Grand Lodge on the condition of the various funds of Grand Lodge and
those of its entities.

The rewarding accumulation of assets in the various funds of Grand
Lodge has been the result of the efforts of M asons and their friends. The
operations of the Masonic Homes at Union City and Covina are financed
almost entirely through the income from th e Endowment and General Fund
investments The expansion at Union City in the last several years was
fund ed entirely from the Hugo M Loerber Fund. Other revenues furnish
support for the charitable activities and educational grants of the Grand
Lodge of California.

During the year. quarterly meetings are held by the committee with its
investment counselors. At each meeting. an independent analytical consulting firm reviews the performance of the various funds as compared with
similar funds, ie .. endowment, non-profit and trust funds. in the United
States.

The Funds' investments rank among the highest in quality because the
Investment Comminee exercises the utmost prudence and care in their
overseeing of the management of the funds. The cash yield and the total
return have been satisfactory in view of the committee's conservative
investment policy and the securities markets

The primary concern of the committee is the preservation of the principal
of the various funds, together with the highest rate of return commensurate
with sa fety and good investment practice.

During the past year a great deal of study has been made regarding the
benefits and flexibility gained with the use of mutual funds rather than
individuall y managed portfolios and some funds have been converted to
that investment medium It is anticipated more will follow Consideration
has also been g iven to the conversio n of funds to equity investments away
from fixed return in the smaller accounts under our responsibility and to the
acceleration of that conversion in the General Fund and the Endowment
Fund.

354

INVESTMENTS

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M of California :

Three funds, Masonic Homes Endowment Fund. Masonic Hornes
General Fund , and the Hugo M. Loerber Union City Fund constitute 91% of
the total investments
Among other funds supervised by the committee are the General Fund
of Gra nd Lodge. the funds of the California Masonic Memorial Temple, the
California Masonic Foundation and Amaranth. Pooled Income and several
restricted-use funds of Masonic Homes.
The market value o f the total po rtfo lio on June 30, 1991, was
$304,006.251 compared to $271,385,400 a year earlier. an increase of 12%.
The 1991 · 1992 cash yield from the total invested funds was 5.6%
Fixed income securities, including government and corporate bonds
composed 58% of the total portfolio. The remainder consisted of common
stocks and cash equivalents.
The Pooled Income Fund is of particular importance to the Masonic
Homes. On June 30 , 192, the Fund had a market value of $4.859,55 1. The
cash yield was 7.02% . The Pooled Income Fund gives Masons and others
in California the opportunity to create a lifetime income for themselves at1'
for one time designated beneficiaries. Certain tax advantages may accrue
to the contributor. Eventually, the Masonic Homes becomes the final
beneficiary.

355

The committee received numerous calls from constituent lodges asking
for investment advice and help regard ing investible funds they have In the
past, the committee has not been in a position to g ive such assistance but
is sensitive to the need. A good deal of time has been spent studying the
creation of a ·pooled ' fund into which lodges might voluntarily place such
money which might grow to a size which would make professional manage .
ment efficient and feasibl e.
During the year, a survey was conducted of all California lodges. The
134 replies received indicated some SS.000.000 which would be available
for such investing. The committee is aware of approximately $3,000,000
additional funds which would go into such a pool from lodges which did not
18ply to the suNey since they had already contacted the committee directly.
The question of whether and how to proceed has been before the Grand

Master and the Executive Committee for several months now with no
decision o n their part.

REPORT OF THE COMMITIEE ON
MASONIC EDUCATION

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California :
The Masonic Ed ucation Committee was reorganized at the time The
Most Worshipful Grand Master. Stanley M. Cazneaux. was proclaimed duly
installed on October 16, 1991 .

361

At the request of the Deputy Grand Master, R. Stephen Doan, a Steering
Committee on Masonic Education, composed of a committeeman from
each Division, was established during the month of July. The members are:
H .L. Rutan, PM, Clayton Valley St. John's Lodge No. 756. Division I; Charles
Trullinger, PM , Northern Light Lodge No. 190, Division II ; John M. Storlie,
PM. Paso Robles Lodge No. 286. Division Ill ; John L. Baker. PM , Mor:iing
Star Lodge No. 68. Division IV: Auri Spigelman. Tri Lodge No. 595, Division
V; Rodney P. Wineberg, PM, Claremont Lodge No. 436, Division VI; Robert
L. Sorensen, PM. El Cerrito Albany Lodge No. 686. Chairman.
Primary purpose of the Steering Committee is to establish a long rc.nge
plan and agenda for the Masonic Education Committee for 1993 and
beyond. Recommendations made at the July 11, 1992, m eeting were as
follows :
A. Continue to use the present Masonic Education Manuals.
Revision Ill . to be included in next printing_
B. Study.and implementation of a Correspondence Course.

The state was divided into 19 Areas. A Masonic Education Committeeman was appointed and assigned to each area. Forty-five Masonic
Education Area Instructors were assigned to assist the committeemen. It
was evident that additional commineem en and Area Instructors would be
needed to effectively implement the use and acceptance of the new Masonic
Education Manuals by the lodges.
In February. additional committeemen were appointed and the Masonic
Education Areas were increased to 26: the number of Area Instructors was
increased to 62. The committee was successful in implementing the new
Masonic Education Program throughout the state. The program was
received with an overwhelming approval rate. There were some lodges that
were not interested and resented Grand Lodge '. .. trying to tell them what to
do."
The committee made arrangements with the Grand Lodge of Illinois to
d istribute the videotape' Unseen Journey." The tape has been utilized very
effectively by many of the lodges and is responsible tor reac hing and
favorably impressing non-Masons and Masons with the goodness and
greatness of our fraternity. The committee will present the Grand Lodge

C. Prepare Masonic Light Award and Pin Program.
D. Request "How To and How Not To" videotapes be prepared by
Video Training Tape Comminee. Using Masonic Skits as a starting
point.
E. Prepare 'Walk-about the Lodge" and Personnel Board papers
for use by the lodges.
F. Assign a Masonic Education Area Instructor to each District to
increase effectiveness of the Maosnic Education Committee.
G. Hold a Masonic Education workshop in each Masonic Education
Area before April 1, 1993.
This year has been a learning process not only for the lodges but also
for the Masonic Education Committee. At the present time, we believe the
foundation for our program is in place and has proven beyond a doubt that

REPORT OF THE COMMITIEE ON

CO MMITIEE MEETINGS

MASONIC SERVICE BUREAUS

Four comminee meetings were held during the 1991 / 1992 Grand Lodge
Year. The first. a two day session on Monday. October 14, 1992 and
Tuesday, October 15, 1991 , during the Grand Lodge sessions. In addition,
a Bureau Booth was manned on Saturday. October 12 through Tuesday,
October 14 on "G" Floor. At this Booth several booklets regarding Masonic
Service Bureau activities and those of the Masonic Service Association.
along with information relating to the Masonic Relief Association of the
United States and Canada were available to those an ending Grand Lodge.

To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The Committee on Masonic Service Bureaus is pleased to submit the
following data as its Annual Report for the Fiscal Year ending June 30. 1992.
The committe is in its 65th year
The committee takes its directive to oversee the operations of the
various Masonic Service Bureaus in the State of California very seriously.
This includes the Rel ief of Distressed Worthy Brothers and their families
residing in the jurisdiction of the Grand Lodge F & A.M. of California, both
sojourners as well as California members, when the situation requires it. The
committee also assists the Masonic Service Association in the development
of its "Hospital Visitation Program" at the Veterans Administration Facilities
in the State of California.
COMMITIEE MEMBERS
The committee underwent a major restructuring during the second half
of fiscal 1991 / 1992. First, the resignation. due to poor health, of Worshipful
Jesse Canale, was received, and subsequently word of his death lost us the
major benefit of his Masonic and Service Bureau knowledge. Shortly after
Jesse's resignation, the Comminee Chairman. Worshipful DeMay "Dee"
White, resigned due to a conflict with his duties as a Grand Lodge District
Inspector.
To fill one of these two committee vacancies, Worshipful Brother Paul
Brown, Coronado Lodge No. 441 . was appointed by the Grand Master to fill
the San Diego opening. Brother Arthur H. Rumsey, Liberal Arts lodge No.
677, was appointed to the committee and subsequently appointed by the
Grand Master to till the position of Committee Chairman.
EXECUTIVE SECRETARIES
Worshipful Birdell Wilson replaced Worshipful Gene Capps as Executive
Secretary of the San Jose Service Bureau, and is in the process of reorganiz ing the Bureau to bring it up to California Masonic Service Bureau Standards.
Worshipful Brother William H. Murphy replaced Worshipful Harry C. Dunlavy, who resigned due to family health problems as Executive Secretary of
the Fresno Service Bureau.

365

The first Regional Meeting of the year was held in Pasadena on April 4,
1992. New committeeman Arthur Rumsey was present. along with representatives from all the Southern California Bureaus. a total of 27 individuals.
At the conclusion of the General Meeting with the Bureau Representatives
and Officers, a business meeting of the committee was held. under the
direction of Chairman Worshipful DeMay H. White. and it was unanimously
voted to retain the six year limitation to continuous service as a Representative. Four of five committeemen were present and participated in this
vote.
The Northern California Regional Meeting was held in Oakland on
Saturday, May 16, 1992, with newly appointed Chairman Art Rumsey at the
helm . Four Bureaus from Fresno-North were represented, plus the San
Francisco Bureau, represented by their Assistant Secretary. Worshipful
Birdell Wilson, the newly appointed Executive Secretary of the San Jose
Bureau. was on hand and was approved by Committee Action. The former
Grand Secretary, Very Worshipful Robert A. Klinger. discussed the newly
established San Francisco Hospital Visitation Program of MSA. which he is
heading
On Saturday, June 15, 1992 the Annual Budget consideration meeting
of the committee was held in Pasadena for the purpose of read ing and
approving the Budgets of al l active Service Bureaus in California. Four of
the five committee members were present. including newly appoi nted Paul
Brown from the San Diego Area.
TAXES
We are advised that all State and Federal Tax returns have been
completed and filed w ithin the allotted time frame

BUSH MASONIC RELIEF, INC.

Brother Crawford is heading the Lodge Beach program as Field Representative : Worshipful Bill Weathers. Senior Represen tative in California. is
working with the Loma Linda Medical Facility near San Bernardino; Worshipful "Dee" White is the Representative at the Sepulveda Facility; Worshipful Theodor e E. Gibbon is Field Representative at San Diego: Worshipful
Royal Dano is the Representative at the Brentwo od Division: and Brother
Arthur Rumsey is the Wadsworth Division Representative at the West Los
Angeles Medical Center. Worshipful A. Bruce Ivie is serving as California
Coordina tor and Volunteer at Sepulveda, and Very Worshipful Bob Klinger
has come on board at the San Francisc o V.A. Facility.

As has been the case for the past many years, the Service Bureaus
located in Los Angeles County, Long Beach, Los Angeles. San Gabriel
Valley, and Sa_nta Monica Bay have been supporte d by the Bush Fund. and
we do appreciate this generous contribution to our activities.

UNIFORM COMPACTS

past year, however. most all have been the results of mergers of lodge
relocations. those which have been approved are as follows:

OPERATING EXPENSES
The total cost of operating the fourteen active Service Bureaus for fiscal
1991-1992 amounte d to $182,883.27. This figure represents a reduction of
$16,898.25 from the $199, 781 .62 approved for fi scal 1991 - 1992. As has
been the past practice. some Bureau Executive Secretaries are salaried
some are strictly volunteers. and some receive a mileage reimbursemen~
only.
M.S.A. HOSPITAL VISITATION PROGRAM
This program, with what this Committee Chairman feels is an imcomplete title. con~inues to move ahead, perhaps slower than hoped for but still
on the up-swing at many of the Veterans Hospitals in California. The
Pro~ram , as done in this state, includes not only Visitations. but also direct
servrce to the Veteran, including Van Driving, Administrative Assistance
Clerical activities. and just about anything needed in the hospital that ca~
be done by Non-Certified individuals.

CONCLUSIONS
The Service Bureau Program in California is faced with a major crisis in
the immedia te future. The Bureaus received unannou nced major increases
in their Liability Insurance Charges. long after their 1992-1993 Budgets were
prepared and subsequently approved by this committee. and have left
several in the position of seriously consider ing dissolution, due to lodge
objection to increased "Per Capita" needed to keep the Bureaus afloat . For
example: the Bakersfield Bureau's total Budget. without Insurance. was
$450.00. The Liability Insurance billing is $266.00. To meet this 50 + %
increase. the Bureau's Per Capita has to go from $0.35 to approxim ately
$0.65 (an inc rease of almost $0.45 in one year) and the 5 lodges belonging
to the Bureau a.re among those considering Bureau dissolution.
A meeting with representatives of the Insurance Committee. and Grand
Lodge office personnel, has been requested in an attempt to retain the
California Masonic Service Bureau structure, in the face of these increasing
costs.
The Committee Chairman has personally visited the San Jose Bureau.
twice: the San Gabriel Valley Bureau twice: and the Bakersfield Bureau for
the purpose of attemptin g to answer some of their questions regarding
where they go from here. The Chairman also visited the San Diego Bureau
for the purpose of presenting a Plaque to the widow of long time committe e
member, Jesse Canale. and attended the Annual Meetings of the Long
Beach and Santa Monica Bay Bureaus, and visits the Los Angeles Bureau
meetings on a regular basis.
We do appreciate the support given to this committee by Most Worshipful Stanley M. Cazneaux, and his corps of elected and appointed officers.
along with several of the appointed Committ ee Chairrnen with whom the
Chairman has had direct dealings during this short period of time. The
Presidents and Officers of each of the Service Bureaus are to be congratu -

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

NECROLOGY REPORT

PAST MASTERS. MASTERS AND WARDENS

Most Worshipful Grand Master. members of the Grand Lodge. distinguished
guests. my brethren all:
From !he Holy Writings·
'Let us now praise famous men: the heroes of our nation's history.
through whom the Lord established HIS renown. all these won tame in their
own generation and were the pride of their times. ·
Since we last met here. in the house of the faithful. 454 members of this
Grand Lodge have enlisted in that greater army whose footfalls make no
sound. Their names will be inscribed in the printed record of this Communication.
As we dedicate this moment to eulogize the lives and actions of these
leaders of Masons. we remember. each one of us. an individual who
contributed to the expansion of our own Masonic life. growth and understanding.

It is the great tragedy. and also the supreme opportunity of human
existence. that the entire body of human thought must be retaught and
relearned by every new generation thus. all of us here today are the products

of the lives and thoughts of those who have gone on to perfection.
It is proper. then. that we may categorize these brethren as having been.
truly, the Three Great Supports of Masonry: WISDOM. STRENGTH and
BEAUTY. It was because of their WISDOM that we have inherited the glory
that is Masonry. It is due to their STRENGTH that Masonry has been passed
on to us, pure and unimpaired. from generation to generation: and as we
reflect upon the BEAUTY of our ancient fraternity. we must realize that it was
given to us by our departed brethren who knew their duty and had the
courage to do it.
It is incumbent upon us. then. the inheritors of their great legacy. to carry
forward the noble work of Masonry: to be numbered among those who best
can work and best agree. to be characterized. in the words of one of our
most beloved Past Grand Masters as 'Those who know how to pronounce
the word right."

EULOGY
SAMUEL ELSTON HARRIS
He was born on May 25. 1923. in Dos Palos. California . Sam attended
public school in Dos Palos. and graduated from Dos Palos High School
When his family moved to Southern California. he attended Pasadena City
CoUege. Sam joined the Army in the early 1940's and on his discharge from
the Army, he attended San Jose State University and graduated with a BA
in Business Administration
Brother Harris married Charlsie Hooton on March 21. 1948, and from
that union. they had 4 daughters. 3 granddaughters and 1 grandson. named
Sammy. the apple of Sam's eye.
Sam worked for the CNA Insurance Company and retired as their Los
Angeles office manager after 34 years of service.
Brother Sam was an active member of the Committee to Incorporate
the City of Temple City during 1959-1960. Sam also served several years
as an Elder for the Westminster Presbyterian Church of Temple City.

REPORT OF THE COMMITIEE ON
POLICY AND GENERAL PURPOSE

Request for Approval, College of the Societas Roscruciana
in Civitatibus Foederatis in California and for
Recognition of the National Society of Blue Slipper Ladies
In 1988, Grand Lodge approved an amendment to Section 2068. California Masonic Code. to permit a Mason residing in this jurisdiction to become
a member of any organization. the prerequisite of which is that a person be
a Master Mason. if that organization existed in this jurisdiction on September
27. 1957. or if, the constitution and by-laws of such oroanization have first
been submitted to the Grand Lodae for approval at a subsequent Annual
Communication.
This request is for the establishment of one College of Roscrucians in
California. The membership in any college is limited to 72. and all would
consist of recognized Masonic scholars and students of philosophy.
The primary purpose of the college. in addition to the conferral of the
several grades under its control, is the advancement of knowledge in the
philosophy and .ethics of Freemasonry. The organization is not a religion ,
and is not connected with other Roscrucian organizations in the United
States It has numbered among its members such illustrious scholars as
the late Manly Palmer Hall. who received some of the highest honors in
Masonry for his contributions to the understanding of the history and
philosophy of the Fraternity.
It appears that the applicants to form a Societas Roscruciana in
Civitatjbus foederatis in California have complied with the essential
provisions of Section 15130 of the California Masonic Code and approval is
in order.

The second request is for recognition, by this Grand Lodge, of the
National Society of Slue Slipper Ladies
This organization was constituted on August 26, 1989 in the State of
New Mexico. It was granted re<::ognition by the Most Worshipful Grand
Lodge of New Mexico in March. 1990. They are now seeking recognition
by the respective Grand Lodges of the United States of America.

382

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

We believe this request is premature. We know of no one in California
who is involved in this organization. Nor do we know of any effort to start
such a club in this State. We also feel that recognition should only be
considered for organizations that have demonstrated by their accomplishments. over a period of time. that they are worthy of endorsement by the
Grand Lodge of California.

1. To study the future relationship between the Grand Lodge of
California and the M.W. Prince Hall Grand Lodge F&AM State of
California. Inc.. and their constituent lodges:

We would also note that, as a matter of general policy, it has been a
concern of this committee that it is possible that the great number of Orders,
Organizations. and Clubs. appendant to this Grand Lodge. may indeed.
serve to the detriment of our Craft Lodges. We need not reiterate the
demands placed upon our most capable Masons due to their multiple
Masonic memberships.

3. To report to the 1991 Annual Communication of the Grand Lodge
of California its Findings.

We make exception to our concern. the instance of the College of
Roscrucians. We therefore recommend that the request for the establishment of one College of the Societas Roscruciana in Civitatbus Foederatis
in California be approved by this Grand Lodge.
We further recommend that the request for recognition by the National
society of Blue Slipper Ladies be denied at this time on the basis that we
find the request to be premature
Respectfully submirted,

REPORT OF THE SPECIAL COMMITTEE
ON PRINCE HALL RECOGNITION STUDY
To the Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& AM. of California:

This special committee was formed in October 1990 at the direction of
the Grand Master as a result of a verbal agreement between the incoming
Grand Master and its proponent when Resolution No. 90-7 was withdrawn
at the 1990 Annual Communication.
The purpose of the committee was:

383

2. To meet with representatives of the M.W. Prince Hall Grand
Lodge of California, Inc. and

At the 1991 Annual Communication of our Grand Lodge the recommendation was adopted that your special committee "be continued for the
1991/92 Masonic year, as presently composed. with the explicit charge to
bring before the 1992 Annual Communication its proposals and recommendations respecting some form of recognition of the M.W. Prince Hall Grand
Hall Lodge of California, Inc. and also that the Grand Master, the Deputy
Grand Master and their designees continue to meet informally with corresponding representatives of the MW. Prince Hall Grand Lodge, Inc." Your
special committee has continued in conformity with that action by t11e Grand
lodge and now reports further.
Since shortly after our formation we have met with a like committee from
the Prince Hall.Grand Lodge. These formally arranged meetings have led
to informal meetings and conversations between members of each committee. We now distribute our formal meeting minutes as a joint committee.
We have all come to a berter understanding of each others words based on
an improved understanding of each others fraternal culture
The joint committee. while in pursuit of its mandates, has recognized
that the Mason of the twenty-first century will be faced with new and
perplexing challenges as never before. He will be forced to think about the
basic principles of Freemasonry and examine his relationship to those
principles. He will be required to justify his actions to the uninitiated
observer. especially if his practices do not align themselves with the lofty
ideals which Masonry proclaims.
It is clear that, as time and social conditions change, so must the
paradigms that we construct change. New situations arise and new solu·
tions must be developed. Your committee recognizes the need to meet the
challenges of the twenty-first century and has taken the initial steps as you
so directed. to be the catalyst through which a dialogue of understanding
with the M.W. Prince Hall Grand Lodge of California. Inc. can take place.

384

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

As the mutual understanding process moves forward, the joint committee acknowledges that a change of relationship and understanding between
the two Grand Lodges in California can only be accomplished through
continued thoughtful and ca reful deliberation of all the issu es. and that any
recommendation that this committee brings to this Grand Lodge must be
one that is solid in its foundation. well reasoned. and one not done in haste.

B. Initial Goals
Three goals were established :
1. Understanding of history and evolution of each ent ity

2. Documentation of joint actions of other Grand Jurisdictions.
This committeo has met four (4) times over the past year during the
months of November, February, March, and August with the similar committee of the M.W Prince Hall Grand Lodge of Cal~ornia , Inc. During our
deliberations, we continued to be impressed with the quality, depth, and
sincerity of the commitment of these men representing Prince Hall.
As a result of these interactions we have concluded that the delegation
representing the M.W. Prince Hall Grand Lodge are men of high moral
standards. excellent c haracter. and are devoted to the ideals of Freemasonry and their fraternity in particular We have further concluded that the M.W
Prince Hall Grand Lodge has similar goals and ob1ectives as those of our
Grand Lodge of California, and that the Prince Hall Grand Lodge subscribes
to the same moral standard s and principles as we do.
In the area of documentation. your joint committee has gathered a vast
amount of written material. involving history, current policy and law. actions
by other Grand Lodges, interpretation of applicable public law. and periodicals. All of which requires careful review. much research. and serious
consideration as it relates to the question of masonic regularity.

3. Understanding of the term "recognition ' and of its meaning and
application as used in Freemasonry.
Dialogue in all three of the above areas is currently on-going. Item 1 is
approaching closure. Items 2 and 3 are still being deliberated upon and
subject to much future study and evaluation.
C. An Agenda For FIJ:!ure Relations
A joint agreement was reached to work toward programs and events
where both Grand Lodges could participate together m a public forum as a
method of building an understanding between the two Grand Lodges. Initial
contacts would be at the Grand Lodge level on ly The Grand Masters would
communicate on an ongoing informal contact basis A tentative schedule
is set to begin thi~ process as follows :

1. October 16, 1992 - Luncheon Meet ing in San Francisco with both
Grand Masters and other designated Grand Lodge Officers.

Discussion in our joint committee meetings is continuing in two very
important areas: namely, the American Doctrine of Exclusive Territorial
Jurisdiction and the California Masonic Code's definition of the term
"clandestine". Our discussions in these two areas must and will continue.

2. The Grand Master of the California Grand Lodge will be the guest
of the Grand Master of the M.W Prince Hall Grand Lodge at the
public religious service of the 138th Annual Communication to be
convened in Los Angeles. CA in 1993

developed the following:

It is believed that after a successful period of time of allowing these and
other Grand Lodge contacts to take place between both entities, a shift to
the subordinate lodges might occur at some level. e.g.: funerals, installations. charitable projects. cornerstones. or the like However, this is not to
be expected in the near term.

Recent policy changes have occurred in the various Grand Lodges and
the sister jurisdictions of the Prince Hall Grand Lodge in Colorado. Connecticut, Idaho. Minnesota. Nebraska . North Dakota. Washington. and Wisconsin who have acted jointly to recognize each others· Grand Lodge in their
res pective jurisdictions. Careful stud y reveals however. that the concept of
· recognition" varies from jurisdiction to jurisdiction. Nevertheless . an itudes

are changing and jurisdictions are accepting the challenge. This development can not be overlooked by each jurisdiction as we approach the

Yet, let it be thoroughly understood that your comminee is fully aware

In closing, we. your special committee on Prince Hall Recognition

TEMPLE PROPERTIES

To rhe Most Worshipful Grand Lodge, F.& A.M. of California:
The Committee on Temple Properties was re-organized this year under
the sponsorship of Grand Master Cazneaux and his Executive Committee.
A pro-active ethic was instilled in the committee to create new avenu~s of
education. new linkages of service to the Craft through the committee
members and the Inspectorate. and greater simplification of the required
reports of financial status of the temple associations to the Grand Lodge
THE COMMITIEE IS RE·ORGANIZED
Two vice-chairmen were appointed as executives for the North and
South parts of the state. Committee members in the North were a.s~i~n~d
specific geographical areas nearest them as their areas of respons1b1htX: m
the South. specific Masonic Districts were assigned to each committee
member. The assignment of specific Masonic Districts close to the home
of the member has proven to be the better plan. The practice will be adopted
statewide next year.
After the committee members reviewed the most frequently requested
subjects of inquiry, specialties were assigned among the members so that
expertise on some subjects is available to lodge members from any part of
the state. As new committee members are added in the future. they will be
chosen not only for mature judgement and geographical location as they
have in the past, but. too, they will be ct1osen for expertise in a subject area
not currently represented on the committee.
During the year. the committee lost by death its former Chairman, David
N. Moeller. added one new member in the East Bay area and added a
consultant in Redding to represent the committee in the extreme Northern
part of the state.
Adequate geographical coverage of the state by the nine-member
committee was called into question during an early brain-storming session
ano it was decided to ask the Grand Master to recommend to the Grand
Lodge at the next Annual Communication that the committee be expanded
to fifteen members. Should such expansion be approved, committee members could be located throughout the state in accordance with the distribu·
tion of the Masonic population of the state. Thus. the services of the
committee might be available within close driving and telephone distance
from each lodge. The burdens of the committee members· job would be

389

FINANCIAL REPORTING IS SIMPLIFIED
Simplification of financial reporting was another goal that came from the
experience of the committee members. It was noted that the detailed nature
of the data requested on Form 200 caused some temple associations to be
reluctant to complete the form and that the detailed information was not
always required for the committee members annual review which is the
purpose of the report. Thus. Form 200 was greatly simplified for distribution
during 1992. A copy of Form 200 is now distributed to the Inspector of the
Masonic District in which the reporting temple association is located
A further simplification ot financial reporting requirements was deemed
warranted when apparently confusing language was discovered in the
California Masonic Code concerning the due date of Form 200 and the
period for which it is prepared. Three members of the committee prepared
a resolution for consideration at the 1992 Annual Communication which
would clarify the period of the report and align the due date to the temple
association's reporting requirements to the Federal and State tax
authorities.
OWNING REAL E:STATE BY THE PROPER FORM OF INCORPORATION
The committee continued its efforts to identify those few temple associa·
tions and lodges not yet in compliance with the California Masonic Code
concerning ownership of real estate. Noncompliers are usually lodges
which own their temples directly (not through an incorporated temple
association) or do not use the proper form of Articles of Incorporation and
Bylaws. Greater compliance was made possible through education of the
Inspectorate and through education of lodge members at the Officers·
Administrative Seminars. The message continued to center on the actual
monetary liability to each individual member of a lodge which chooses to
own its real estate directly. the requirement of the federal tax law that a
nonprofit fraternal 501 (c} (10) organization own real estate through a
501 (c)(2} corporation and that a lodge's insurance coverage and rates are
based upon an expectation by the insurance carrier that the lodge does not
own the real estate directly.
These arguements were found to be most persuasive in convincing
those few noncomplying lodges to properly incorporate their real estate
holdings. When lodge members were caused to think about how much and
in how many ways our society has changed during the past one hundred
years. they readily see why the method of ownership of real estate used one

PROCEEDINGS OF THE

GRAND LODGE OF CALIFORNIA

hundred years ago is no longer adequate for the lodge. or its members,
today . 'We've always done it that way successfully· is really another way of
saying. We'll do it the old way until we lose a lawsuit and learn the hard way
that the world has changed!

EDUCATION
Training meetings were held for the committee members to help transfer
k nowledge and standardize responses to questions on many sub1ects
Presentations were made at each Divisional meeting of Inspectors to better
explore the supportive relationship between the committee and the Inspectors. All of the Officers' Administrative Seminars had a commiltee member
present to provide an introduction to our services to Masters and Temple
Association Directors.
Individual committee members made themselves available to speak
before temple associations or lodges at the request of Inspectors. Masters
or Tempie Association Presidents.
COMMUNICATION
An expanded version of the Temple Properties Committee Handbook

was published and reformu lated several times during the year. The expan ·
sion allowed tor several subjects to be addressed concerning the background concepts behind many of the California Masonic Code sections and
accepted Grand Lodge regulations Content of the Handbook was changed
several times reflecting commentary tram users and review by the committee members. The expanded version has nearly unanimous positive acceptance by lodge members.
Plans for a quarterly newsletter to temple associations progressed, but
the first number of the planned publication has not yet been mailed. A
database of names and addresses of temple associations and their presidents was compiled from several sources. including lists created by the
Inspectors and the mailing list of the major insurer of temple associations in
the state.
Greater care was exercised during the year to inform all interested
parties of the subjects and nature of inquiries to the members of the
comminee . Typically. this meant that the commiltee member sent a sum mary letter of a personal or telephone conversation to the lodge member
who initiated the contact. Copies of the Ien er were then addressed to the
local Inspector. the Master and the President of the Temple Association, as
deemed appropriate. These added steps of communication seemed to
mitigate many brewing controversies by informing all possible parties of an
unanswered question.

391

Several California Masonic Code sections require committee approval
of certain acts of temple associations and lodges. During the year the
committee sought to create nore ett1cient and timely oversight of such acts.
Primarily, temple associations were asked to inc.;lude the Temple Properties
Committee dialogue earlier in their planning process than previously had
been the norm. Thus. less delay occurs at the final stages of the approval
process because the Temple Properties Committee membership and the
Executive Committee. if necessary, have become familiar with the background and details of the proposition.
Because the Inspectors were made aware of our wish to reduce delay
through this procedure. a much greater volume of inquiry came to the
committee on questions of taxation, California Masonic Code regulation and
procedure, seismic rehabilitation, Masonic cemeteries, asbestos removal.
oil product contamination and leases
The members of the Comminee on Tempie Properties were heartened
by the acceptance of their seNices by Inspectors. Masters and Temple
Associations. We look forward to greater use of our services in the years to
come.
Respectfully submitted .
C. Richard Hutchinson. Jr., Chairman
